Now, on `create_order`'s keyed branch (`orders/views.py`, the `if replay is not None` block): a guest submission must ALSO satisfy `_same_purchase` - the same cart lines, coupon and complete six-field shipping payload the keyless dedup guard already required, extracted into one helper both guards call. A disagreement returns **409** `{"error": "This Idempotency-Key was already used for a different checkout submission"}` (wrapped by the house `ErrorEnvelopeMiddleware` into `code: "conflict"`, `details: {}`) - no order body, no token, no existence signal beyond the key the caller supplied. 409 rather than a silent second order because the `(guest_email, idempotency_key)` constraint forbids minting one; letting the INSERT fail would have been a 500.
- **BUG-1, the second half: the credential is disclosed on the MINT and nowhere else.** `_checkout_response` now takes `disclose_guest_token` and the only caller passing `True` is the 201 creation (`HTTP_201_CREATED`). Both collapses - the keyless dedup and the keyed replay - pass `False`. This is the defence-in-depth half and it is deliberately INDEPENDENT of any collapse guard: even if `_same_purchase` were wrong or deleted, no response but the mint carries the token. **Stated cost:** a guest whose 201 never reached their client gets the order from a retry but not the credential, so they cannot read that order by token - the price of not making a permanent credential replayable, on a path whose order is unpayable anyway while guest payment remains an open spec gap.
- **The agreement requirement is scoped to the guest branch, and that scoping is the argument, not a convenience.** An authenticated caller proved who they are at login, so `(user, key)` is a strong pair and SPEC-9.3.14's "a keyed replay collapses regardless of payload drift" still stands for them - `test_an_account_keyed_replay_still_tolerates_payload_drift` and the four drift-tolerance pins in `orders/test_idempotency.py` all still pass unchanged. A guest proved only an email and a guessable key.
- **BUG-2 (P2), what is now refused, and where.** `POST /api/orders/checkout/`, anonymous branch, before the validator and long before the INSERT: a `guest_email` wider than 254 characters returns **400** `{"error": "guest_email must be at most 254 characters"}`. `validate_email` accepts any length and SQLite does not enforce `varchar(254)`, so the pre-fix endpoint answered 201 and stored the address verbatim - a `DataError` 500 on the production Postgres. The limit is read off `Order._meta.get_field("guest_email").max_length`, never a literal, so the refusal and the column cannot drift. The pin is BEHAVIOUR (400 + `Order.objects.count() == 0`, plus the exactly-254 boundary still accepted), not the field declaration the cycle-1 test asserted.
- **BUG-2, extended to the same code path in the same statement, declared:** `full_name` (150), `phone` (15), `city`/`state` (100) and `pincode` (10) had the identical hole on the identical path (a loop over `_SHIPPING_FIELDS` immediately after the required-field check, before the atomic block opens), and `address` is a `TextField` so `_field_length_rejection` returns `None` for it by design. All six now 400 on the account path too - the loop is not guest-only.
- **BUG-3 (P2), what changed.** `_canonical_guest_email` strips surrounding whitespace and `casefold`s at the boundary, in `_resolve_checkout_owner`, before the row exists. `VICTIM@EXAMPLE.COM` no longer creates a second order beside `victim@example.com`, so one buyer has one addressable row and the `(guest_email, idempotency_key)` conditional constraint is an authority across spellings. `casefold` over `lower` because the value is only ever a scoping and matching key, where folding two spellings together is always the safe direction and the equality classes never split. `strip()` also closes the trailing-space variant of the BUG-1 repro.
- **BUG-3 required NO migration, stated explicitly.** What is stored changed (the canonical form), what the schema holds did not - no field, no constraint and no index moves, so `makemigrations --check --dry-run` reports "No changes detected" (measured, exit 0). A data migration to re-canonicalise existing rows was considered and REJECTED: two rows differing only by case that share an idempotency key would collide on the conditional unique constraint during the backfill, and the feature has never shipped (this branch was never pushed), so there are no stored rows to migrate.
- **BUG-4 (P3), and the cycle-1 claim it corrects.** Comparing Black's hunks against the ADDED-line set - not against the pre-change file state, which only proved the FILES were already dirty - found added lines Black would rewrite in `orders/models.py` (`guest_email`'s `default`, the `related_name`, both constraint `fields`/`name`/`Q(...)` groups), `orders/serializers.py` (both `'guest_email'` field-list entries), `orders/urls.py` (the two guest `path(...)` calls), `orders/views.py` (`@throttle_scope('cart')`, the guest lookup's `@api_view(['GET'])`) and `orders/tests.py` (one over-88 call). All are conformant now; re-measured with the same checker, **0** lines this task adds are rewritten by Black. Django-generated migrations are excluded and stay as `makemigrations` wrote them, which is the state of 28 of the 31 migration files already in the repo.
- **Tests: 9 added, and each one is shown to BITE by mutating the fix and watching it fail, then reverting.** `test_a_stolen_email_and_key_buy_the_attacker_nothing` (the audit's exact repro), `test_the_trailing_space_variant_of_a_stolen_email_buys_nothing`, `test_a_keyed_replay_with_a_drifted_payload_is_refused`, `test_a_collapse_never_hands_back_the_credential`, `test_a_keyed_guest_resubmission_collapses_onto_the_first_order` (rewritten to the new contract), `test_an_over_length_guest_email_is_refused_before_the_row_exists`, `test_an_over_length_shipping_field_is_refused_before_the_row_exists`, `test_the_guest_email_is_stored_in_one_canonical_form`, `test_a_case_variant_of_the_guest_email_is_one_owner_not_two`, plus `test_an_account_keyed_replay_still_tolerates_payload_drift` to pin the scoping. Mutation evidence, each applied to `orders/views.py`, run and reverted: (1) `if user is None and not _same_purchase(...)` -> `if False and ...` => 3 failures (the three BUG-1 repro tests); (2) `if disclose_guest_token and order.user_id is None:` -> `if order.user_id is None:` => 2 failures (both token-on-collapse assertions); (3) drop the `guest_email` length gate => 1 failure; (4) drop the shipping-field length gate => 2 subTest failures (`full_name`, `phone`); (5) `casefold()` -> `strip()` => 2 failures (canonical form, case variant).
- **Two assertions changed, not deleted, and both because the cycle-1 contract they pinned is the defect the audit found.** `test_an_identical_resubmission_collapses_onto_the_first_order` and `test_a_keyed_guest_resubmission_collapses_onto_the_first_order` each asserted `guest_token` present on a collapse, and the second drove its replay with a DELIBERATELY DRIFTED payload (`city="Pune"`) to prove SPEC-9.3.14's drift tolerance. Drift tolerance for a guest is now a 409 (pinned by `test_a_keyed_replay_with_a_drifted_payload_is_refused`) and the collapse carries no credential (pinned by `test_a_collapse_never_hands_back_the_credential`); the identical-payload keyed collapse is still pinned, and the account path's drift tolerance is pinned separately. Nothing was weakened, skipped or excluded.
- **Untouched, deliberately:** `create_payment` / `verify_payment` stay account-scoped and unauthenticated-token-free - guest payment is a SPEC GAP the audit parked for its own money-path task, and reaching it here would have been scope creep onto the money path. No change to the reservation or oversell behaviour, to the token entropy (`secrets`, 32 bytes, unique), to the byte-identical 404, to the env-driven IP-keyed `cart` throttle scope, or to the B03 staff-surface scoping. No new staff capability. Files touched: `orders/views.py`, `orders/models.py`, `orders/serializers.py`, `orders/tests_guest.py`, `orders/urls.py`, `orders/tests.py`, this changelog - every one inside the declared scope, and no new migration.
- Suite before (measured at `c1b181a`, this worktree): **1400 tests, OK (1396 pass + 4 pre-existing expectedFailure - unchanged)**, coverage **100.00%** (5948 stmts, 0 miss), exit 0. Suite after, measured at the tip of this cycle: **1409 tests, OK (1405 pass + 4 pre-existing expectedFailure - unchanged)**, coverage **100.00%** (6071 stmts, 0 miss), exit 0; `makemigrations --check --dry-run` "No changes detected", exit 0.

## 2026-10-01 - SPEC-1-B04 FIX CYCLE 3 (Section 1, [R-1.13]) - builder: BUG-5 (P2), the account-path half of the shipping-width pin was vacuous

- **BUG-5 (P2), what the test actually did.** `orders/tests_guest.py::test_an_over_length_shipping_field_is_refused_before_the_row_exists` reached its account half with `self.auth(self.api_login("buyer")[1])` and NO preceding `make_user` / `register_and_verify`. No such user existed, so the login 401'd, `api_login` returned `(res, None)`, `auth(None)` CLEARED the credentials on the shared `self.client`, and the "account" submission was ANONYMOUS. It then answered 400 on the `guest_email is required` gate - a different gate, in a different function - and the block only asserted `status_code == 400`, so it passed for a reason that had nothing to do with the width gate. The suite log shows the tell: two `audit auth.login_failed ... detail={'username': 'buyer'}` events, `actor=None`, one per subTest.
- **What it is now.** The account half authenticates for real, using the helpers the neighbouring tests in the same file already use (`self.make_user("buyer")` + `self.api_login` + `self.auth(token)`), and holds a cart of its own via `seed_session_cart` - without one the request would have answered 404 on the cart gate, which is a second unrelated 400-shaped way to pass. Both halves now assert the gate's MESSAGE (`f"{field} must be at most {limit} characters"`, the exact string `_field_length_rejection` builds) and not merely the status, so a request that 400s anywhere else fails the test. Each half also asserts `Order.objects.count() == 0`, so a row created and then rolled back cannot satisfy the count. `auth(None)` moved out of the loop: the credential is set once for the pair and cleared once, rather than re-authenticating inside every subTest.
- **Why the message assertion and not the status is the fix.** The status does not identify which gate fired: an anonymous submission of this same payload answers 400 on `guest_email is required` (`_resolve_checkout_owner`) and a cartless caller answers 404 on the cart gate, while the width gate lives in a third place again (`create_order`, after the required-field loop). Only the message names the gate uniquely. Cycle 2's mutation evidence for this test was "drop the shipping-field length gate => 2 subTest failures" - true as run, but it only ever exercised DELETING the gate; the escape hatch BUG-5 is about SCOPING it back to the guest branch, which deleting it cannot detect.
- **Mutation evidence, both directions, `orders/views.py` reverted after each (git status shows it unmodified).** Wrapping the `_SHIPPING_FIELDS` loop in `if user is None:` - the exact re-scoping that keeps the production defect's cause alive - makes the corrected test **FAIL** with **2 subTest failures** (`full_name`: `AssertionError: 201 != 400`, the account path having stored a 151-character name; `phone`: `AssertionError: 1 != 0`, the count catching the row the first subTest leaked), exit 1. The SAME mutation against the pre-fix test is **OK, exit 0** - which is the defect stated as a measurement, not an argument. Reverted and re-measured: **OK, exit 0**. No production file is in this commit.
- **Correction to the cycle-2 wording above, now that it is true rather than merely asserted.** Line-for-line, cycle 2 wrote "All six now 400 on the account path too - the loop is not guest-only", listed this test among those "each one is shown to BITE", and carried an in-code comment saying the account path shares the gate. The production claim was correct (the auditor verified the code independently) but until this cycle NO test proved it: `grep "must be at most"` matched only the two guest-path assertions, `tests_guest.py` guest_email and the guest half of this test. Those two claims now have the measurement behind them and are left standing. Cycle 2's stated mutation for this test is re-read above rather than rewritten; its own text remains accurate for the mutation it describes.
- **Every other `api_login` call site in the file was audited and is unaffected.** The file has **7** `api_login` call sites and all 7 now have a `make_user` on the line immediately above (`:164`, `:240` - the one fixed here - `:574`, `:742`, `:877`, `:1021`, `:1050`). The other 6 were already correct and are untouched. No other block in the guest suite asserts a bare status on a request whose body it has not pinned, and no other half of this test passes for an unrelated reason.
- **Suite before (measured at `33f1f1f`, this worktree): 1409 tests, OK (1405 pass + 4 pre-existing expectedFailure - unchanged), coverage 100.00% (6071 stmts, 0 miss), exit 0.** Suite after, measured at the tip of this cycle: **1409 tests, OK (1405 pass + 4 pre-existing expectedFailure - unchanged), coverage 100.00% (6076 stmts, 0 miss), exit 0**; `makemigrations --check --dry-run` "No changes detected", exit 0; `orders.tests_guest` alone 53 tests OK exit 0. The statement count moves 6071 -> 6076 because `.coveragerc` sets `source = .` and omits only `tests/*` and `*/tests.py`, so `orders/tests_guest.py` is itself measured (525 stmts, 100.00%) and this cycle adds 5 statements of real setup to it. Test COUNT is unchanged at 1409 by design: this cycle rewrites one existing test rather than adding one, so nothing is deleted, skipped, weakened or xfailed - the expectedFailure set stays at 4.
- **Scope: `orders/tests_guest.py` and this changelog, nothing else.** `orders/views.py` is not touched - the production behaviour is correct and was proved so independently. No migration, no env key, no new test, no deleted assertion.

## 2026-10-01 - SPEC-1-B05 (Section 1, [R-1.07]) - builder: shipping methods carry rates, and the cost they price is computed and applied server-side at checkout

Spec 6.9 lines 1999-2005 names "Shipping zones", "Serviceable postal codes/regions", "Flat-rate and weight-based shipping" and "Free-shipping rules"; line 4155 names "Zones, rates, carriers, restrictions" under settings; spec 9.1 line 2703 declares `GET /store/shipping/estimate`; and the storefront mock at line 939 shows shipping as "Calculated", not chosen. The requirement: shipping methods exist with rates, and the CORRECT cost is computed server-side and folded into the order total.

- **The app choice, and why: a NEW `shipping` app, because the codebase has no home for order-shipping concerns.** Nothing shipping-related exists to extend - `orders/` holds the order/payment/refund lifecycle and its admin surface is the B03-audited `orders.state` machine this task must not widen. Spec 6.9 treats shipping as its own admin domain (`/admin/shipping`), and SPEC-1-B06 (Shipment + ShipmentEvent) will land beside these two tables, so one app is the home for both rather than a second one later. `shipping/models.py`, `serializers.py`, `views.py`, `urls.py`, `admin.py`, `apps.py`, `__init__.py`, `migrations/0001_initial.py`, `tests.py`.

- **TWO models, where the ledger's sizing said 1 - declared, with the argument.** The task's own requirement is "a shipping method CARRIES rates", and spec 6.9 line 4155 names "rates" as their own entity, so `ShippingMethod` (the client's pick) and `ShippingRate` (what it costs for one geography) are two tables with a real FK. A single table would have to fake the method/rate distinction with a grouping column and could not express a method offered at two prices. `orders` gains two COLUMNS, not a model.

- **The geography is exactly what the spec and the order carry: region + postal-code prefix. No country column, no zone table, invented neither.** `Order` has `state` and `pincode` and nothing else geographic (spec lines 920-933 collect City/State/PIN), so a `country` column on the rate could never match a stored destination and would be decoration. An empty `region`/`postal_code_prefix` is the WILDCARD, which is what lets a national rate exist without a special case, and prefix matching is what the spec's "serviceable postal codes" and the storefront's "pincode checker" (line 657) both mean. `UniqueConstraint(method, region, postal_code_prefix)` keeps one amount per method per geography, so two rates can never compete for one destination invisibly.

- **Weight-based rates are deliberately NOT modelled, and the reason is a fact about the catalogue rather than a scope decision.** Spec 6.9 line 2003 names them beside flat rates, but `products.products` carries `size` (the bottle volume) and no weight in grams anywhere, so a weight band would have nothing to match against. It belongs to the first task that gives a product a weight. Free-shipping rules (line 2005) ARE delivered, as the env-driven threshold below.

- **ONE resolver, two callers - `shipping/pricing.py` (a file beyond the declared list, declared).** The estimate endpoint and checkout share it, which is what makes requirement 7 structural rather than a promise: a destination cannot price one way on the estimate and another at checkout, and the guest and account paths price identically because they are the same view calling the same function, not because two copies agree. It also holds the only place a rate is turned into an amount.

- **Two orders of precedence, for two different questions, and I got the first one wrong before the tests caught it.** INSIDE one method the NARROWEST matching rate wins over the cheapest (a regional rate exists to override the national one); ACROSS methods the CHEAPEST wins, because a customer who expressed no preference is choosing by price and must not be handed the expensive option merely because a store prices it only for their region. Both pinned (`test_the_narrower_rate_wins_even_when_it_is_the_pricier_one`, `test_an_unpriced_preference_picks_the_cheapest_method_not_the_narrowest`).

- **THE security property: the client may send an IDENTIFIER and never an amount.** Checkout reads `state`, `pincode` and the optional `shipping_method` CODE, hands them to `quote_shipping`, and stores the server's answer. `shipping_amount`, `shipping_cost`, `shipping` and `total_amount` have NO reader anywhere on the path - there is no field to write them into, because `Order.objects.create(...)` is fed only computed values. Pinned four ways: a hostile POST with `shipping_amount: 0.00` still persists `shipping_amount == 49.00` and `total_amount == 1048.99`; the same for the three other forged names and for a forged `total_amount`; the response body carries the server's numbers, not the posted ones; and `test_the_price_is_the_gateway_charge_and_the_refund_ceiling` shows the delivery charge reaching Razorpay and the refund ceiling, since both read `total_amount` and neither had to learn about shipping. **Mutation evidence:** making the view read `request.data['shipping_amount']` turns 2 of the 3 `ClientSuppliedAmountTests` red; reverting turns them green again.

- **No matching rate is a REFUSAL, not a free shipment and not a 500 - the decision, stated and pinned.** Shipping configured but nothing serving this destination returns **400** `Shipping is not available for this destination. Please check your delivery address.`; a named method that does not resolve returns **400** with its own message (a bad code and an unserviceable address are different corrections for the customer). Both refusals roll the whole checkout back: no order, no order items, no stock reservation, no status event, no audit row, and no burnt order number. Fallback-to-a-default-rate was rejected: it converts a serviceability fact into revenue loss, and the loud answer is the one the storefront can render.

- **The ONE silent zero, isolated and named: a store with NO shipping configured.** No active `ShippingMethod` means shipping was never switched on, which is a different fact from "we do not ship there", so checkout records `shipping_amount = 0.00` with `shipping_method = NULL` - an explicit zero on the row, not a rate of zero standing in for a price nobody could compute. This branch is also what keeps the 1405 delivered tests green (they all check out with no shipping configured); refusing there would have regressed the entire suite. `shipping_configured()` exists to keep the two facts apart, and `test_a_store_with_no_shipping_configured_records_a_real_zero_charge` pins the branch.

- **The cost is priced and written INSIDE the checkout's atomic block, and the refusal is caught OUTSIDE it.** `create_order`'s `transaction.atomic()` is now wrapped in a `try` whose only handler is `ShippingUnavailable`, so a refusal PROPAGATES out of the block and rolls it back whole rather than being answered from inside it (a `return` from inside an atomic block commits whatever the block already wrote - and an order with no shipping cost IS a free shipment). Pinned three ways: a spy on the pricing call records `connection.in_atomic_block == True` and `lock=True`; the `select_for_update` flag is asserted on the Query that is actually evaluated (`[True, False]`, present for checkout and absent for the estimate - SQLite emits no FOR UPDATE, so the flag is asserted where it exists); and failing the audit hook - the LAST writer in the block - leaves 0 orders, 0 items, 0 reservations, 0 status events and 0 ORDER_CREATED rows, with the next real checkout still numbered `ORD-2026-000001`.

- **Pricing runs AFTER both collapse guards, so a retry is never repriced or refused.** The cost is resolved after the keyless dedup guard and after the keyed replay probe. That ordering is what keeps `test_the_keyed_replay_of_an_unserviceable_destination_still_collapses` green: a rate retired between two submissions of the same payload still collapses onto the original order instead of turning the retry into a 400.

- **Requirement 6 - B04's dedup agreement: the delivery option JOINS it, and the reason it does not break it.** `_same_purchase` asks a fifth question, `_same_shipping_choice`: a submission that NAMES a method must name the one the order was priced with (so a keyed guest replay naming a different option is a **409**, not another order's body - tightening the B04 guard), while a submission that names NOTHING agrees, because the server chose it. The keyless double-click still collapses with shipping in the payload (`test_the_keyless_double_click_still_collapses_with_shipping`), so the accidental-duplicate window survives. And a collapse returns the ORIGINAL order with its own persisted `shipping_amount`/`total_amount`, which is the structural reason no replay can change a total - pinned by `test_a_forged_amount_on_a_replay_cannot_change_the_total`.

- **Money is Decimal end to end, and the float-hostile cases are pinned as exact strings.** `_charge` quantizes with the shared `common.money` helper before anything compares or serializes; checkout adds `quantize_money(merchandise_total + shipping_amount)`. `test_a_float_hostile_rate_produces_an_exact_decimal_total` (0.10 x 3 plus a 0.20 rate) asserts the stored total is the Decimal `0.50` AND the serialized value is the string `"0.50"`, where a float anywhere in the chain yields `0.30000000000000004`; `test_a_two_digit_total_is_exact_to_the_paisa` asserts `20.20 + 10.10 == "30.30"`. The estimate serializes amounts as decimal STRINGS, never JSON numbers, because a JSON number is a double in the browser.

- **The free-shipping rule is env-driven (`SHIPPING_FREE_THRESHOLD`), never a literal.** `_env_money` in `config/settings.py` reads it as a string and quantizes it through the same helper every amount uses (a threshold that only agreed with the amounts to within a float's precision would misfire on round totals); unparseable and negative values fall back to "the rule is off" with a warning rather than taking the app down or making every shipment free; the key is documented in `.env.example`. The rule compares the DISCOUNTED MERCHANDISE total, so a coupon that takes goods below the threshold brings the charge back - pinned end-to-end (`test_the_threshold_compares_the_discounted_merchandise_total`). Rates themselves are NOT config: they are staff rows, because they change per geography far more often than a deployment changes its env.

- **`GET /store/shipping/estimate/` - the one public read, anonymous deliberately, and a RATE QUOTE rather than a total.** It returns each serviceable method priced for the destination (`method` code, `name`, decimal-string `amount`, `free_shipping`), plus the store currency, and never a row id. `AllowAny` is stated at the class because a guest checks out without an account (spec line 74) and would otherwise not see a delivery price; it is safe to open because the body is a public price list for a destination, with no cart, order, customer or staff data in it. No throttle scope, because the convention requires one on public MUTATING endpoints and this only reads. Both destination fields are REQUIRED rather than one being treated as a wildcard: checkout always has both, and resolving with only one could tell the storefront a destination is unserviceable when it is not. The estimate reports the PLAIN rate - it has no cart to read - so checkout stays the single authority for what is charged and the client is never asked to compute money.

- **Least privilege: `shipping.manage` follows the MONEY, not the packing operator.** Granted to `{finance, admin}`, exactly as `refunds.create` is, because a rate is a price every future order will be charged. Spec 1.1 line 110's "shipping" IS delivered to the inventory/fulfilment role - as `orders.fulfill`, the authority to ship an order (SPEC-1-B03) - but WORKING a shipment and SETTING the prices it will cost are different powers, and granting it to the operator would have broken `tests/test_superadmin_tier.py`'s exact-set pin on that role (a decision B03 shipped after three audited cycles), so the capability does not widen it. Support is excluded for the same reason it holds no `refunds.create`. All four permission kinds map to it: reading the rate table is part of managing it, and no shipping row holds customer data. Pinned both ways: the inventory operator is refused 403 at both admin changelists, a finance user reaches both and the change form saves a rate that then prices a destination.

- **Retiring a method cannot reprice or hide an order.** `Order.shipping_method` is `SET_NULL` (PROTECT would make the admin's delete view raise on referenced rows - a 500 on a legitimate action), and the money record is the AMOUNT, which no operation can rewrite: pinned by `test_retiring_a_method_does_not_reprice_or_hide_an_order`. The amount and the label are separate columns for exactly this reason.

- **`common/permissions.py::HasShippingManage` is a file beyond the declared list, declared, and it is not optional.** `tests/test_rbac_foundation.py` pins one named permission class per capability in `CAPABILITY_ROLES` (and no pinned class outside it), so adding the capability without the class is a red suite - proven by running it, not assumed. It is the named gate for the shipping admin surface; no customer-facing endpoint uses it.

- **Scope declared beyond the ledger's 5 files / 1 model:** `shipping/pricing.py` (the shared resolver), `orders/serializers.py` (the checkout/order body must expose the method code and the amount, read-only, or the client cannot see what it was charged), `orders/migrations/0018_*` (mechanically forced by the two new `Order` columns - the B04 precedent), `common/roles.py` + `common/permissions.py` (the RBAC seam and the class its invariant test requires), `.env.example` (the new key), `shipping/migrations/__init__.py`. **Deliberately untouched:** `orders/admin.py` (adding shipping columns to the order change form would have touched the B03-audited scoped-write surface for no requirement) and `orders/state.py` (requirement 8 forbids widening it).

- **74 new tests, all in `shipping/tests.py`** (omitted from coverage by the repo's `.coveragerc` `*/tests.py` rule, like every other app's): rate selection (wildcard / region / prefix / full code / case and space / inactive rate / inactive method / specificity / price tie-breaks / the two wrong answers to "which rate"), the two refusals and the unconfigured store, the free-shipping rule, the estimate (parity, decimal strings, no row id, legacy alias, missing fields, partial serviceability, unconfigured), the checkout cost (recorded on the order and in the ORDER_CREATED audit detail, coupon interaction, free shipping, gateway charge), the forged-amount property, guest/account parity, Decimal precision, atomicity (three), the replay agreement (six), the admin seam (six) and the env reader (six).

- **Suite before (measured this worktree at `017f46c`): 1409 tests, OK (1405 pass + 4 pre-existing expectedFailure), coverage 100.00% (6076 stmts, 0 miss), exit 0.** Suite after, measured in this worktree: **1483 tests, OK (1479 pass + 4 pre-existing expectedFailure - unchanged), coverage 100.00% (6266 stmts, 0 miss), exit 0**, `makemigrations --check --dry-run` clean ("No changes detected", exit 0). Black: the whole new `shipping/` app is Black-clean; the legacy files are not Black-formatted at all (reformatting `orders/views.py` wholesale would be a 5000-line drive-by), so every line this task added to them is 88-col and matches the surrounding style.
## 2026-10-01 - SPEC-1-B05 FIX CYCLE 2 (Section 1, [R-1.07]) - builder: BUG-1 (P2) a NaN threshold crashed settings import; BUG-4/5 (P3) a hard-deleted delivery option erased itself from historical orders, and the unconfigured-store zero was silent

Fix cycle 2 of max 3. The money path itself was not re-litigated and nothing in it changed: the amount is still priced server-side, still a Decimal, still applied inside the checkout's atomic block, and `shipping_amount` is untouched by every change below.

- **BUG-1 (P2) is REFUSED at the source, not patched for one input.** `config/settings.py::_env_money` wrapped only `quantize_money(Decimal(raw))` in its `try`, then ran `if value < Decimal("0.00")` OUTSIDE it. `Decimal("NaN").quantize(Decimal("0.01"))` returns a QUIET NaN without raising, so `SHIPPING_FREE_THRESHOLD=NaN` passed the parse and the comparison then raised `decimal.InvalidOperation` - i.e. the process died at settings import, which is the exact failure the function's own docstring and `.env.example` promise cannot happen. The function now tests `value.is_finite()` INSIDE the `try` and refuses on three explicit, individually-logged grounds, each resolving to the same SAFE direction (rule off = every shipment still charged): unparseable or non-finite, negative, and above `_MONEY_MAX` (`Decimal("99999999.99")`, the largest amount `DecimalField(max_digits=10, decimal_places=2)` can hold - a threshold beyond it is a typo, not a policy).
- **The whole non-finite family is now refused, measured input by input, not assumed.** `NaN`, `nan`, `-NaN` previously CRASHED (quantize returns a quiet NaN; only the comparison raises). `sNaN`, `Infinity`, `-Infinity`, `inf` and a 40-digit value were already caught by `quantize` raising inside the `try` - verified, not assumed - and are now refused by the same explicit guards instead of by that one implementation detail. `test_no_unusable_value_is_ever_answered_with_an_unorderable_amount` pins the PROPERTY over all twelve hostile spellings rather than the spelling, and `test_the_largest_storable_threshold_is_still_honoured` pins the boundary the new ceiling draws (`99999999.99` is still a legal threshold).
- **BUG-4 (P3): a hard-deleted delivery option no longer erases itself from the orders that used it. Approach: a denormalized label snapshot on the order, and the reason is that this codebase already ships that exact pattern for the same spec clause.** Spec 8.3 line 2497 ("Orders must retain what was actually purchased") is already answered on this model by `OrderItem.product_name`: a `SET_NULL` FK to a catalogue row PLUS a frozen label beside it, and that pairing is what survives a catalogue change. `Order.shipping_method` was the FK half with no snapshot half, so deleting a `ShippingMethod` cascaded its rates, `SET_NULL`ed the FK, and left `shipping_method: null` on a historical order - a body indistinguishable from an order priced while the store had no shipping configured at all. The new `Order.shipping_method_code` (CharField(40), `blank=True`, `default=''`, written once at checkout from `ShippingQuote.method_code`) is the `OrderItem.product_name` half for the same requirement.
- **BUG-4: why not the other two routes.** A `PROTECT` FK would block the delete outright and turn a legitimate admin action into a 500 on referenced rows - the shipped comment at `orders/models.py` already rejected it, and the audit instruction was not to change the shipped `SET_NULL` semantics; it is unchanged, and `test_deactivation_keeps_the_order_and_the_label_intact` plus `test_hard_deleting_the_method_keeps_the_label_on_a_historical_order` pin both halves together (the FK does go null, the label does not). Refusing delete when referenced was rejected as the primary fix because it protects the label only while the code path is the admin: a shell, a data migration or a future cascade still erases it, whereas a snapshot is unconditional. Deactivation remains the documented retirement path and now has the snapshot behind it.
- **BUG-4: the snapshot is READ, not just stored, because an invisible record is not a preserved one.** `OrderSerializer` gains an explicit, read-only `shipping_method_code` beside `shipping_method` and `shipping_amount`, so after the method row is deleted the order body reads `shipping_method: null, shipping_method_code: "standard", shipping_amount: "49.00"` and still names what was bought. `test_a_deleted_option_is_tellable_apart_from_never_configured` is pinned at its sharpest: with the free-shipping rule on, the deleted-option order and the never-configured order agree on BOTH `shipping_method` (null) AND `shipping_amount` (0.00), and the label is the only thing left that tells them apart.
- **BUG-5 (P3): the zero charge is UNCHANGED, and it is no longer silent. Three signals, none of which alters what is charged.** (1) `orders/views.py` logs a WARNING naming the submission, the cause ("no active shipping method configured") and the fix (`manage.py seed_shipping_methods`), one line per order placed - a rate an operator can act on. It fires on the silent zero ONLY: `test_a_charged_shipment_logs_no_such_warning` and `test_a_refused_destination_logs_no_such_warning` pin that a priced shipment and an unserviceable-destination refusal stay quiet, so the signal cannot decay into noise. (2) `ops/services.py::get_health` reports `checks["shipping_configured"]` beside `smtp_configured` and `razorpay_mode` - the EXISTING deployment-configuration health surface extended, not a new one invented. It is deliberately NOT part of the probe's `status` computation: a store that has not configured shipping is a pre-launch state, not an outage, and gating `status` would turn a fresh deployment into a 503 and mail the staff mailbox on every poll. `test_unconfigured_shipping_does_not_make_the_probe_degraded` pins that choice, including `/health/` still answering 200. (3) `manage.py seed_shipping_methods` (`ops/management/commands/`) is the fix for the root enabler: two national methods, each with one geography-wildcard rate, created ONLY by an operator running it. No data migration, no fixture, no import-time side effect - `test_importing_the_command_writes_nothing` pins the last one, because a data migration would put a made-up price in every fresh database.
- **The seed command is idempotent and can never reset a live price.** It plans in a first pass of pure reads (so `--dry-run` writes nothing - a dry run built from `get_or_create` would be a lie about a command whose only job is writing) and executes in a second. An existing method is FETCHED, never updated, so a merchant's own name and `is_active` flag survive and a retired method is not silently reactivated; an existing rate is never repriced. Amounts are CLI options validated as finite non-negative 2-dp Decimals BEFORE any write, and a bad flag raises `CommandError` rather than storing a bad price - unlike an env default, where a typo must not stop the operator.
- **BUG-2/BUG-3 (P3): the two formatting defects are fixed and the file set is measured, not asserted.** `common/roles.py:65` had the first line of the `shipping.manage` comment at column 0 inside the `CAPABILITY_ROLES` literal (lines 66-78 at 4) - now 4, and `black --check` reports the file unchanged. `orders/views.py:922` had the `[R-1.13]` collapse comment at 12 spaces with its block body at 16, escaping the `if duplicate is not None:` block it documents - now 16. Black does not reformat comments, so the second one was swept by hand across all ten files this cycle touches: a scan of every added comment line against the indent of the next code line reports **0** drifts.
- **Black status of the 1183926 file set, measured at the pre-task head `5d42952` and at `017f46c` before it.** `common/roles.py` was clean at `017f46c`, was the only newly-failing file at `5d42952`, and is clean again. `shipping/` is still 11/11 clean. The two migrations and the new command are clean. SIX files - `config/settings.py`, `config/urls.py`, `orders/models.py`, `orders/serializers.py`, `orders/views.py`, `ops/services.py` - already failed `black --check` at `017f46c`, i.e. before this task touched them; they carry pre-existing single-quote/indent debt and were deliberately NOT reformatted here, because a whole-file reformat of the money path would swamp this diff. Every line this task ADDED is Black-clean, and the claim is scoped to added lines only: the old-side line numbers Black emits as REMOVED in `black --diff` - i.e. the lines whose text Black actually rewrites, not the wider hunk ranges, which over-count because a hunk is dirty when any line in it is - intersected with this task's added line numbers, file by file, reports **0** over `5d42952..HEAD` for all 11 touched `.py` files. The six files above still FAIL `black --check` as FILES, and are not claimed to be clean: their remaining debt is entirely on pre-existing lines that were already dirty at `017f46c` and that this task neither added nor touched, and no whole-file reformat was performed. (Correction, audit cycle 3: this bullet previously said "no other added line of mine is reformatted" on the strength of a `git diff --no-index` against a `black`-formatted copy, which was FALSE - that method compares whole files and so cannot separate a debt line from an added one, and it had missed `orders/models.py:310`, where this task added `default=''` inside the new `shipping_method_code` field. That line is now `default=""`; the per-file intersection above is the check that actually supports the claim, and the earlier `git diff --no-index` self-verification is retracted.)
- **Mutation evidence, every one reverted and the file re-measured after.** (1) `usable = True` in place of the finiteness guard -> `shipping.tests.ShippingThresholdSettingTests` **5 errors**, every one `decimal.InvalidOperation` (the reported crash, reproduced), exit 1. (2) the `roles.py` comment restored to column 0 -> `black --check` "would reformat"; reverted -> "1 file would be left unchanged". (3) the `views.py` comment restored to 12 spaces -> measured indent 12 against a block body of 16; fixed -> 16. (4) `shipping_method_code = ""` at the checkout write -> `ShippingMethodDeletionTests` **5 failures**, each `AssertionError: '' != 'standard'`, exit 1. (5) `if shipping_quote is None and False` plus `checks["shipping_configured"] = True` -> `UnconfiguredShippingSignalTests` **4 failures**: the two log tests "no logs of level WARNING or higher triggered on orders.views", the two health tests `True is not false`, exit 1. (6) the command file moved away -> `CommandError: Unknown command: 'seed_shipping_methods'`, file restored.
- **32 new tests, all in `shipping/tests.py`** (omitted from coverage by the repo's `.coveragerc` `*/tests.py` rule, like every other app's): 9 threshold-refusal tests, 6 delivery-option-deletion tests, 7 unconfigured-signal tests, 10 seed-command tests.
- **Scope, declared beyond the ledger's list.** `ops/services.py` is the BUG-5 health surface the instruction named ("extend the existing settings/admin health surface rather than inventing one") and there is no other; `ops/management/commands/seed_shipping_methods.py` is the command the instruction authorised, placed in the existing `ops` command package beside `backup_db`/`restore_drill`/`email_send_probe` rather than creating a new `management/commands` package tree under `shipping`; `orders/serializers.py` is where the BUG-4 snapshot becomes readable. `orders/migrations/0019_order_shipping_method_code.py` is mechanical (one `AddField`). The new tests went into `shipping/tests.py` rather than `tests/test_settings_security.py` because `shipping/tests.py::ShippingThresholdSettingTests` is the class that already pins `_env_money` with the same `_env` helper, so the BUG-1 tests sit beside the behaviour they extend; the BUG-5 health assertions joined them because the thing under test is the SHIPPING state being visible.
- **Suite before (measured this worktree at the pre-task head `5d42952`): 1483 tests, OK (1479 pass + 4 pre-existing expectedFailure), coverage 100.00% (6266 stmts, 0 miss), exit 0.** Suite after, measured at the tip of this cycle: **1515 tests, OK (1511 pass + 4 pre-existing expectedFailure - unchanged), coverage 100.00% (6327 stmts, 0 miss), exit 0**; `shipping` alone **106 tests OK, exit 0**; `makemigrations --check --dry-run` "No changes detected", exit 0. The statement count moves 6266 -> 6327 for the new command, the new health helper, the `_env_money` guards and the checkout snapshot write; the expectedFailure set stays at 4 and no existing test was deleted, skipped, weakened or xfailed.

## 2026-10-02 - SPEC-1-B05 FIX CYCLE 3 (audit cycle 3 of max 3, FINAL) - builder: BUG-6 (P3), one `black` restyle in a line this task added, and a changelog claim that overstated its own verification

Fix cycle 3, the last of the three this task was allowed. One line of code, one sentence of changelog. No shipping
logic, money path, snapshot behaviour, warning/health signal, seed command or migration was touched - cycle 2 closed
all six of those and the auditor re-verified them clean, so there was nothing to relitigate.

- **BUG-6 (P3), the one defect: `backend/orders/models.py:310` now reads `default="",` where it read `default='',`.**
  It is a line cycle 2 ADDED, inside the new `shipping_method_code` CharField, and it is the only line in this
  task's diff that `black` rewrites. Quote-style only: the value, the field, the blank default and the migration
  are all identical, so no behaviour, no serialization output and no stored row changes. The migration
  `0019_order_shipping_method_code` embeds the field definition and is deliberately NOT regenerated - it was
  written when the line already had this meaning, and rewriting a shipped migration for a quote character would
  be a lie about what was applied. `makemigrations --check --dry-run` reports "No changes detected", which is the
  proof that the model and the migration still agree.
- **The 106 files of pre-existing Black debt in this repo are NOT touched, and the fix is not a reformat.** Six
  of the files in this diff still fail `black --check` as FILES and did so at `017f46c`, before this task began;
  that debt is on lines this task neither added nor modified. Reformatting `orders/models.py` whole would have
  rewritten 432 lines of unrelated money-path code (`black --diff` on the file reports 432 old-side lines against
  347 new-side ones), burying a one-character fix under a diff the audit has to read, so the change is one line.
- **The cycle-2 changelog row contained a FALSE claim, and a false self-verification is a defect even when the
  code it describes is correct.** Cycle 2 asserted "no other added line of mine is reformatted", backed by
  `git diff --no-index` of the file against a `black`-formatted copy. That method is incapable of supporting the
  claim: a whole-file comparison cannot tell a line this task added from a line that was already dirty, so a
  newly-added line inside an already-dirty region is indistinguishable from the surrounding pre-existing debt -
  which is exactly how `orders/models.py:310` slipped through. The claim is corrected in place above and the
  `git diff --no-index` self-verification is retracted there, not quietly dropped.
- **The replacement check is stated precisely, and the method was validated by reproducing the auditor's number
  before trusting it.** The measure is the set of old-side line numbers `black --diff` emits as REMOVED (the lines
  whose text Black actually rewrites), intersected per file with the line numbers this task's diff ADDS. Two
  coarser measures were tried and REJECTED as over-counting, and the reason is worth recording so the next agent
  does not re-invent them: taking a hunk's whole old-side range counts every line in a hunk that is dirty because
  any OTHER line in it is dirty (measured 11 for `orders/models.py` instead of 1), and diffing the worktree instead
  of the committed content measures the wrong file entirely once the fix is applied (it reports 0 for the very line
  under dispute, because the worktree no longer has the defect). Run against `5d42952..b2ebe1a` the accepted
  method returns **5 Black-dirty files and an intersection of exactly 1, at `orders/models.py:310` `default='',`** -
  the auditor's figures, reproduced before the fix was trusted. Run against `5d42952..HEAD` after the fix it
  returns **the same 5 Black-dirty files and an intersection of 0.** The dirty-file count not moving is the point:
  the fix removed the debt on the line this task added and left the pre-existing debt exactly where it was.
- **No test was added, deliberately.** A quote character has no runtime behaviour to assert; a test pinning
  formatting would assert on the formatter, not on the product, and would fail the moment the repo's Black debt is
  paid down. The formatting guarantee is verified by the measurement above, which is reproducible from a clean
  checkout, rather than by a test that launders a style preference into a regression guard.
- **Suite: unchanged at 1515 tests, OK (1511 pass + 4 pre-existing expectedFailure), coverage 100.00% (6327 stmts,
  0 miss), exit 0; `makemigrations --check --dry-run` "No changes detected", exit 0.** Measured before the edit
  and again after it, on the full suite, because a one-character style change is exactly the kind of change that
  is assumed to be inert. The expectedFailure set stays at 4, the statement count stays at 6327, and no test was
  added, deleted, skipped, weakened or xfailed. Nothing in this cycle is a behavioural change, so no behavioural
  test moved - the numbers are identical before and after, which is the expected result and is reported as such
  rather than dressed up as new coverage.
- **Scope: `backend/orders/models.py` (one line) and `backend/docs/changes.md` (this section, plus the corrected
  sentence).** No other file was modified, and no shipping logic, money path, snapshot behaviour, warning/health
  signal or seed command was re-read into change.
## 2026-10-02 - SPEC-1-B06 (Section 1, [R-1.08]) - builder: a shipment carries a tracking reference and an append-only event trail, staff work it through the RBAC admin seam, and a customer tracks their own order by order number

Spec 6.9 names "Shipment creation" (line 2009), "Tracking number" (line 2013) and "Tracking events" (line 2015) under the shipping-and-fulfilment admin domain and gives the route `/admin/shipments` (line 1991); spec 10.2 line 3465 gives the shipment its own lifecycle dimension ("Label created, dispatched, in transit, delivered, exception"); spec 3.9 lines 1185/1189 give the customer route `/track-order` and define its flow as "a secure, limited-access token or authenticated account"; spec 6.9 line 2027 says what that page must not expose; spec 10.3 lists an "Audit record" as the last element of every transition's contract; spec 8.3 line 2565 prescribes an index on the "Shipment tracking reference". The requirement: a shipment exists with a history of events, staff can see and update it, and a customer can track their order by order number.

- **The app choice, and why: the `shipping/` app B05 built, not a new one and not `orders/`.** B05's own changelog entry records the decision that B06 would land beside its two tables rather than open a second app, and spec 6.9 treats them as one admin domain. `orders/` was rejected for the reason requirement 8 gives: `orders/state.py` is capability-scoped by SPEC-1-B03 and audited, and a shipment vocabulary has no business inside that machine. Files: `shipping/models.py`, `serializers.py`, `views.py`, `urls.py`, `admin.py`, `migrations/0002_shipment_shipmentevent.py`, `tests_tracking.py`.

- **TWO models, where the ledger's sizing said 1 - declared, with the argument.** The ledger anticipated one model; `Shipment` and `ShipmentEvent` are two tables with a real FK because spec 6.9 lists "Tracking number" (2013) and "Tracking events" (2015) as two separate features and spec 10.3 demands a per-transition audit record. One table cannot hold both "where this parcel is now" and "every place it has been" without a second column set per row, which is the modelling error that makes a trail unappendable. They are tightly coupled (an event is meaningless without its shipment, and cascades with it), so this is one feature split in two tables rather than the two features the sizing implied.

- **The status vocabulary is spec 10.2's five words verbatim, and nothing else.** `label_created / dispatched / in_transit / delivered / exception` - line 3465, in the spec's own order, and none of the vague states line 3487 warns against ("success", "done"). `exception` is the spec's own catch-all and the state line 2017 means by "Delivery failure handling".

- **NO shipment transition machine, and that is a decision rather than an omission.** Spec 10.2 gives the states and 10.3 asks each transition to declare its edges, but the spec states no shipment edges anywhere - and inventing a ladder would have (a) made `delivered` terminal, so an operator could never correct a typo'd status on a parcel the carrier had already delivered, and (b) created a second state machine next to the one requirement 8 forbids touching. So every status change is accepted, and the EVENT TRAIL is the record of what happened and in what order - which is exactly what spec 6.9 line 2015 asks for. `orders/state.py` is byte-for-byte untouched, and the shipment vocabulary is never pushed through the order machine.

- **THE security property: an order number alone discloses NOTHING, and the proof is seven probes that all get ONE byte-identical answer.** `_trackable_shipment` resolves two credentials and the order number is never one of them: an AUTHENTICATED caller is scoped to `order__user=request.user` (their own account orders, and structurally no guest row, so signing in is never a way to read a guest order by number); an ANONYMOUS caller must present the B04 `X-Guest-Order-Token` value, matched against the STORED token, so a hit is by definition the order that token was minted for. `UniformMissTests` asserts **byte-identity of the raw response content** (not merely matching status codes, because a differing body is as good an oracle as a differing code) across: no credential at all, a wrong token, an over-wide token (>64, B04's width cap, rejected before it reaches the index), **a token valid for a DIFFERENT order**, an unknown order number, an authenticated stranger, and - the two that a naive filter leaks - an order the caller OWNS that has no shipment yet and a guest order with a valid token and no shipment. All seven are `404 {"error": "Shipment not found"}` via the single `_tracking_lookup_miss()`, 404 rather than 403 for the same reason B04 chose it: "forbidden" confirms the order exists. An order number is a guessable per-year sequence and is never the credential, so brute-forcing one in a million earns the caller nothing.

- **The customer body is six keys, and the omissions are the point.** `ShipmentTrackingSerializer` exposes `order_number, status, carrier, tracking_number, estimated_delivery, events` and nothing else - no delivery address, no recipient name, no money - because spec 6.9 line 2027 requires the page to expose neither "internal carrier credentials" nor "warehouse notes". That promise is kept STRUCTURALLY, not by remembering: the one free-text field on the whole feature is `Shipment.internal_note`, staff-writable and absent from the serializer, and `ShipmentEvent` has NO free-text field at all, so the trail itself has nowhere for a warehouse note to be typed. Pinned by asserting the exact key set, every field's `read_only` (a ModelSerializer is writable by default, so the declared `order_number` carries `read_only=True` explicitly and the model-derived fields ride `read_only_fields`), and that the note text and the street address are absent from the raw response bytes.

- **Spec 3.9's route-contract counterpart is honoured by shape: the customer credential is B04's, not a new one.** The header name and the 64-character cap are repeated in `shipping/views.py` rather than imported, so this app's view layer never depends on the orders app's view layer (`orders.views` already imports `shipping.pricing`; a `shipping -> orders.view` import would close a two-way edge between them). `SerializerContractTests` pins both constants equal to `orders.views`'s, so the duplication cannot drift. No throttle scope: the convention requires one on public MUTATING endpoints and this only reads - the same reasoning B04's and B05's read endpoints use.

- **`shipments.read` / `shipments.write` on {support, admin}, and the least-privilege argument.** Spec 6.9 files "View shipment and tracking history", "Create shipment" and "Add tracking number" (lines 1749/1757/1759) under the ADMIN ORDER ACTIONS - ordinary order work, which is the support role's ("Handle customer enquiries and permitted order issues", line 92) and admin's. The inventory/fulfilment operator is deliberately NOT granted it: spec 1.1 line 110's "shipping" is already delivered to that role as `orders.fulfill` (SPEC-1-B03), and widening it would break that task's exact-set pin. Read and write are separate capabilities so a future read-only support role can take the trail without the pen, and a shipment row is the one shipping row that reaches a CUSTOMER's address by association (through its order), which is why `shipping.manage` did not get them for free. The top tier is not granted either, so SPEC-1-B03's `TOP_TIER_CAPABILITIES` pin is untouched.

- **Requirement 5 - the SPEC-1-B03 scoping seam is not bypassed, and the pin is asserted rather than restated.** Neither `ShipmentAdmin` nor `ShipmentEventAdmin` declares `scoped_view_capability`, so the packing operator's `orders.fulfill` opens no second door onto shipments. `test_the_packers_exact_capability_set_is_unchanged` imports `INVENTORY_CAPABILITIES` from `tests/test_superadmin_tier.py` and compares, so a future widening of `shipments.*` into that role fails in THIS task as well as in the one that shipped the pin - and `test_the_packers_orders_fulfill_opens_no_second_door_onto_shipments` proves the operational consequence (403 on both changelists).

- **Requirement 4 - the RBAC foundation's permission-class pin is satisfied, and the file was declared up front.** `tests/test_rbac_foundation.py` pins one named permission class per capability in `CAPABILITY_ROLES` (and none outside it), so `common/permissions.py::HasShipmentsRead` / `::HasShipmentsWrite` are mandatory the moment the capabilities exist, not optional. `LeastPrivilegeTests` additionally pins `HasShipmentsRead.capability == "shipments.read"` and the matrix against the map. `common/roles.py` + `common/permissions.py` are the only files beyond the ledger's list, and both are forced by requirement 4 (the B05 precedent).

- **Requirement 7 - the tracking write is atomic, does not mutate money, and the rollback is pinned.** `ShipmentAdmin.save_model` reads the previous status under `select_for_update()` INSIDE its own `transaction.atomic()` block, then writes the row and appends the `ShipmentEvent` in that same block, mirroring `OrderAdmin.save_model`. `test_the_status_and_its_event_commit_together` makes the event writer raise and asserts the status did NOT move and no event exists - the persisted row is the contract, as B03 found. `MoneyIsUntouchedTests` drives a real status move on an order carrying B05's `shipping_amount`/`shipping_method_code`/`shipping_method` snapshot and a discounted total, then asserts all four are byte-identical afterwards, and that the tracking response carries no money at all.

- **The trail is append-only and timezone-aware.** `ShipmentEvent.save` rejects any pk-set re-save and `ShipmentEventAdmin` is view-only with `has_add_permission`/`has_delete_permission` returning False, so even Django's superuser bypass cannot create or delete history. `actor` is `SET_NULL` (deleting a staff account must not cascade into the trail) and every timestamp is `auto_now_add` under `USE_TZ`; `test_the_trail_records_every_transition_with_an_aware_timestamp` asserts `tzinfo is not None` on each. `tracking_number` is UNIQUE, which is both the spec 8.3 line 2565 index and the reason one carrier reference cannot answer for two parcels (pinned with an `IntegrityError`).

- **A creation event means a creation THROUGH THE ADMIN, and a row minted straight through the ORM records none.** `old` is None on the add form, so the admin's create records the shipment's opening step; an edit that leaves the status alone writes nothing, so the trail records transitions rather than clicks (pinned both ways). An event means a status CHANGED, and nothing changed at creation on a direct ORM write.

- **50 new tests, all in `shipping/tests_tracking.py`** (a new module rather than the 1561-line `shipping/tests.py`, matching the `orders/tests_guest.py` / `orders/tests_refunds.py` house split). Unlike `shipping/tests.py`, this module is NOT excluded by `.coveragerc`'s `*/tests.py` omit pattern, so its own 327 statements are inside the measured total and report 100.00% (0 miss) like every other file. Model contracts (6), serializer contracts (3), the uniform-miss probes (9), ownership (4), guest end-to-end (5), the staff surface (8), least privilege (6), the event trail (7) and money-untouched (2) - 50 methods, counted with `manage.py test shipping.tests_tracking --verbosity 2`.

- **Suite before (measured in this worktree at `a8c430a`): 1515 tests, OK (1511 pass + 4 pre-existing expectedFailure), exit 0.** Suite after: **1565 tests, OK (1561 pass + 4 pre-existing expectedFailure - unchanged), coverage 100.00% (6770 stmts, 0 miss), exit 0**, `makemigrations --check --dry-run` "No changes detected", exit 0. - **Black: the eight HAND-WRITTEN Python files this task added or edited are clean.** `black --check shipping/models.py shipping/admin.py shipping/serializers.py shipping/views.py shipping/urls.py shipping/tests_tracking.py common/roles.py common/permissions.py` reports 8 unchanged, exit 0. Two of them (`shipping/serializers.py`, `shipping/tests_tracking.py`) needed formatting on first pass and were formatted; `git diff -U0 a8c430a a2f541f` measures **ELEVEN** pre-existing lines removed anywhere in this task, and the enumeration is: **3 module-docstring first lines** (`shipping/admin.py`, `shipping/models.py`, `shipping/serializers.py`, each re-tagged to name [R-1.08] as well), **5 module-docstring BODY lines in `shipping/serializers.py`** (that docstring was reworded to describe two requirements rather than one), and **3 import lines** (`shipping/admin.py`, `shipping/urls.py`, `shipping/views.py`) extended to bring in the new names. The figure originally recorded here was "three module-docstring first lines and four import lines" - seven lines, and wrong twice: there are three import lines, not four, and the five reworded body lines were counted nowhere. Nothing was whole-file reformatted and the repo's Black debt was not touched. The load-bearing half of the claim - that none of this task's ADDED lines is Black-dirty - is TRUE, and was re-measured in cycle 2 by the method SPEC-1-B05 cycle 3 established: the OLD-side line numbers `black --diff` emits as REMOVED, intersected per file with the NEW-side line numbers this task's diff ADDS, returns **0 Black-dirty pre-existing files touched and an intersection of exactly 0**, which agrees with the 8-file `black --check` above (8 unchanged, exit 0). The only `.py` in the range that `black --check` refuses is the generated migration, left in Django's generator shape on purpose. The NINTH file is the generated `shipping/migrations/0002_shipment_shipmentevent.py`, deliberately left in Django's generator shape (single-quoted `fields=` lists) because every pre-existing migration in the repo is in that same shape - `black --check` flags `common/migrations/0002..0005` for the same reason, so formatting this one alone would make it the odd file out.

- **Scope declared beyond the ledger's 5 files / 1 model:** `shipping/serializers.py` (the customer contract needs DRF serializers with explicit fields - conventions.md forbids `'__all__'`, and a hand-built dict would be a worse answer), `shipping/migrations/0002_shipment_shipmentevent.py` (the migration), `shipping/tests_tracking.py` (the tests), `common/roles.py` + `common/permissions.py` (forced by requirement 4). **Deliberately untouched:** `orders/models.py` (the reverse accessor `order.shipments` needs no column and the ledger's "relation if needed" turned out not to be needed), `orders/state.py` (requirement 8), `orders/admin.py` (autocomplete would have meant editing the B03-audited order search fields; a plain order select is used instead, and the reason is in the ModelAdmin docstring), `config/urls.py` (`shipping.urls` is already dual-mounted at `/api/v1/store/shipping/` and `/api/shipping/`, so the tracking route rides both with no new mount).

## 2026-10-02 - SPEC-1-B06 FIX CYCLE 2 (audit cycle 2 of max 3) - builder: six findings - the append-only claim was one layer short of the code, a split order reported one parcel, the deletion policy was implicit, an over-wide value saved on SQLite and raised on Postgres, the credential probes used synthetic ones, and this changelog miscounted its own formatting enumeration

Audit cycle 1 (`a2f541f`, ledgered at `aa38d79`) found two P2s and four P3s. This cycle closes all six. **The credential contract, the uniform-miss contract, the URL shape, every money field, the B05 `shipping_method_code` snapshot, `orders/state.py` and the B03 capability pins are unchanged** - what changed is how much the code can be made to do behind them, and how honestly it says so.

- **BUG-1 (P2), the append-only guarantee was asserted one layer short of what the code enforced. It is now enforced on BOTH write paths.** Cycle 1 shipped a `ShipmentEvent.save()` guard that rejects a pk-set re-save and called the trail "append-only, no code path can rewrite a recorded step". A `save()` guard cannot see the ways a row in that table changes that never call it: `ShipmentEvent.objects.filter(...).update(...)` writes columns straight to SQL, `.delete()` removes them, `bulk_create()` inserts rows with no model method running at all, and `bulk_update()` looks like an object method but is not - Django 6.1 compiles its values into a `CASE` expression and issues it through `queryset.filter(...).update(...)` (verified against the installed source, not assumed). `ShipmentEventQuerySet` now refuses all four with `ValueError`, mirroring the `common.models.AuditEvent` precedent this repo already sets, and `ShipmentEvent.delete()` is a real guard rather than an inherited `Model.delete()`. What is still NOT claimed, and is now stated in the model docstring rather than left for a reader to assume: the admin registration is view-only, and deleting a SHIPMENT takes its trail with it (see BUG-3).
- **`bulk_update` is refused EXPLICITLY rather than left to Django's internal route, for a reason about the caller rather than the guard.** A refusal raised from inside Django's `transaction.atomic(savepoint=False)` wrapper marks the caller's transaction as needing a rollback, so a guard that a caller could catch and log would leave that caller unable to run another query at all - `test_a_bulk_update_refusal_leaves_the_caller_able_to_carry_on` would raise `TransactionManagementError` instead of returning a count. Both querysets therefore raise before that block opens, which also means the guarantee does not depend on a Django implementation detail staying where it is. `ShipmentQuerySet.bulk_update` is scoped like its `update` sibling: `status` in the field list is refused, any other field list is allowed through the same width gate.
- **BUG-2 (P2), a split order reported one parcel - and `.first()` picked the worst one. THIS IS A BREAKING CHANGE TO THE RESPONSE SHAPE, declared as such.** Spec 6.9 line 2021 asks for "Split shipments, if needed" and spec 10.1 line 3419 says an implementation "must explicitly handle split shipments". `_trackable_shipment()` returned a single row via `.first()`, which under `Shipment.Meta`'s newest-first ordering answered with the parcel that left LAST: an order whose first parcel was already delivered reported the still-moving one and never mentioned the delivery. `_trackable_shipments()` now returns every visible parcel, `ShipmentTrackingView` wraps them in `{"shipments": [...]}`, and the order is `("created_at", "id")` so the timeline reads in dispatch order rather than the admin grid's newest-first. **A client written against cycle 1's bare parcel object must read `body["shipments"][0]` instead of `body`; the miss body is untouched and still one shape.** The parcel object itself did not change - `ShipmentTrackingSerializer` still carries exactly its six keys (`order_number, status, carrier, tracking_number, estimated_delivery, events`) with exactly their sensitivity, every one of them a per-parcel fact, so nothing was hoisted into the envelope or dropped out of the parcel to make room for it, and the read-only declaration and the URL are untouched.
- **BUG-3 (P3), the deletion policy existed but was not recorded, so the CASCADE read as an oversight. It is now a decision with its price attached.** Spec 8.3 line 2485 asks for an "explicit deletion/archival policy" and `Shipment.order` is CASCADE - deliberately, and asymmetrically with `ShipmentEvent.actor`'s SET_NULL. A staff account is an ATTRIBUTION on a row that must outlive it; the order is the row's SUBJECT, and this store hard-deletes orders as a matter of course (`Order.user` is itself CASCADE, the backup/restore drill truncates the order table, and `Shipment.__str__`, the changelist's `order` column, `search_fields` on `order__order_number` and the tracking endpoint's own owner filter all join to it, so PROTECT would turn ordinary deletes into crashes). Stated cost: an order's fulfilment history does not outlive the order, which differs from `AuditEvent.order`'s SET_NULL and is accepted only because spec 8.3's archival half is unbuilt. `test_the_deletion_policy_is_the_one_the_model_documents` pins both halves so neither can change silently.
- **BUG-4 (P3), a value wider than its column saved cleanly here and raised `DataError` in production.** SQLite does not enforce `varchar(n)` while Postgres does, so an over-length write was a 500 on the real database and a green test here - the same SQLite/Postgres recurrence already recorded twice in this task's own history. `_reject_oversized_values` now runs in BOTH models' `save()` and in `ShipmentQuerySet.update()`, so it covers the object path and the bulk path. It covers the five bounded columns and nothing else: `Shipment.tracking_number` (100), `Shipment.carrier` (60), `Shipment.status` (20), `ShipmentEvent.from_status` (20), `ShipmentEvent.to_status` (20). `internal_note` is a `TextField` with no declared width and is therefore not gated, which is correct rather than an omission. The bound is read off the field (`max_length`) instead of restated as a literal, so the refusal and the schema cannot drift apart. **The gate measures `str` only, and that is a correction made in this cycle**: it arrived as a `value is None` check, which meant any non-string reaching the same line was passed to `len()` - and since Django routes `bulk_update`'s values through the same `QuerySet.update` as a `Case` expression, the gate crashed a legitimate bulk write with `TypeError: object of type 'Case' has no len()` (and would have done the same to any `F()` update). An expression's width is the database's business, so it is now left alone, and `test_a_computed_value_is_left_to_the_database_not_measured` pins that.
- **BUG-5 (P3), the security probes used credentials that could not fail, so they asserted the test client rather than the product.** `force_authenticate` assigns `request.user` directly and bypasses every authenticator in `DEFAULT_AUTHENTICATION_CLASSES`; a probe built on it passes whether or not the credential contract works. Every ownership and stranger probe now mints a REAL JWT by POSTing to the login endpoint and presents it as a bearer: a stranger holding a valid token for their own account is refused, a forged bearer is refused by the auth stack itself (401, before the view runs, and identical whether or not the order exists), a bare session cookie is not a credential here (the session authenticator is not installed), and a token whose account has been deleted stops working. The uniform-miss matrix was widened from 7 shapes to **13 in a single assertion**, spanning all three credential classes (no credential / guest token / bearer) plus session-only, each of the authenticated shapes against BOTH an existing and an absent order number.
- **The miss contract survived the envelope change intact, and that was the thing most at risk from BUG-2.** `test_every_miss_shape_is_one_shape` asserts **byte-identity of `response.content`**, not of the status code, across all 13 shapes - including the two a naive filter leaks ("the caller OWNS this order but it has no parcel", for both the account and the guest credential) and "a stranger's split order, so the multi-row path cannot become the one shape that answers differently just because it looks at more rows". All 13 are the single `_tracking_lookup_miss()` body. A new raw-bytes sweep asserts the HIT body too, because a key-set assertion is blind to a value leaking inside a key nobody thought to check: for both credential classes the rendered bytes carry no address, city, recipient name, phone, email, guest token, `internal_note`, actor username or money.
- **Mutation evidence, because a test that passes before and after a fix proves nothing.** Each fix's inverse was applied and the corresponding test observed to fail, then reverted: removing `objects = ShipmentEventQuerySet.as_manager()` -> `AppendOnlyGuardTests` 9 failures across its 10 tests (7 methods fail; `test_the_refusals_name_the_model_and_the_reason` reports 3 subtest failures), and `test_the_event_object_itself_cannot_be_deleted` still PASSED, which is the point - the object guard never covered the queryset path; `.order_by(...).first()` and a list replaced by a single row -> `SplitShipmentTests` 7 errors in 8; `CASCADE` -> `PROTECT` -> `ModelContractTests` 1 failure + 1 error in 8; both `_reject_oversized_values(self)` calls removed -> `WidthGateTests` 5 failures in 13 (the bulk-update gate survived, as designed); the `**owner` filter dropped from the lookup -> 9 failures in 102, naming the exact leaked shapes ("wrong token", "token for another order", "authenticated stranger"). This cycle's own additions were mutated the same way: both `bulk_update` overrides renamed out of the MRO -> 1 failure + 3 errors in 18; the `isinstance(value, str)` check reverted to the inherited `value is None` -> 2 errors in 21. BUG-6 is prose and has no runtime surface; its claim is reproducible from a clean checkout instead. **Every count in this list was re-measured from a clean tree in audit cycle 3 (base `0db42de`), each mutation re-applied and reverted rather than trusted:** three figures here are corrected - this bullet's append-only count and the width-gate denominator, both of which this entry previously understated, and the owner-filter denominator, which was measured against a 96-test module that now holds 102 - and the remaining four (7 errors in 8, 1 failure + 1 error in 8, 1 failure + 3 errors in 18, 2 errors in 21) reproduced exactly as first recorded.
- **The guards were checked for over-blocking, because a blunt refusal that breaks a legitimate path is its own defect.** `ShipmentEventQuerySet.delete()` is refused wholesale, so the CASCADE had to be verified empirically rather than assumed: `test_an_order_carries_its_shipments_and_cascades_their_trail` and the new `test_deleting_the_shipment_still_takes_its_trail_with_it` both delete the parent and assert the trail went with it, cleanly and with no exception. Django's delete collector issues its own SQL and never routes through `QuerySet.delete()`, and the `SET_NULL` field update never routes through `QuerySet.update()` - the model docstring says so, and two passing tests now say it too.
- **Least privilege is unchanged and re-verified.** `git diff a8c430a a2f541f -- common/roles.py` adds exactly two capabilities, `shipments.read` and `shipments.write`, both `frozenset({ROLE_SUPPORT, ROLE_ADMIN})`. The packing operator's `orders.fulfill` opens no door onto shipments (403 on both changelists), `INVENTORY_CAPABILITIES` imported from `tests/test_superadmin_tier.py` still compares equal, and `common/` is untouched by this cycle. No role gained anything in cycle 2.
- **Suite: 1609 tests, OK (1605 pass + 4 xf), 100.00% (7080 stmts), exit 0 as inherited - the cycle-1 tree had never been executed.** After this cycle's eight added tests: **1617 tests, OK (1613 pass + 4 pre-existing expectedFailure - unchanged), coverage 100.00% (7155 stmts, 0 miss), exit 0**; `makemigrations --check --dry-run` "No changes detected", exit 0 (no new migration - every change here is Python-level); `black --check` on the same 8 files reports 8 unchanged, exit 0; `manage.py test shipping.tests_tracking` is 102 methods (model contracts 8, serializer contracts 5, uniform miss 11, ownership 10, guest end-to-end 5, split shipments 8, staff surface 8, least privilege 6, event trail 8, money untouched 2, append-only guards 10, status-not-movable-in-bulk 8, width gate 13). Cycle 1's own "50 new tests" figure is superseded by this count.
- **Scope for this cycle:** `shipping/models.py`, `shipping/views.py`, `shipping/serializers.py`, `shipping/urls.py`, `shipping/admin.py` (docstrings and the two guards), `shipping/tests_tracking.py`, `backend/docs/changes.md`. **Deliberately untouched:** `common/roles.py`, `common/permissions.py`, `orders/`, every migration, `backend/docs/spec-run-state.md` and `backend/docs/run/`.
## 2026-10-02 - SPEC-1-B07a (Section 1, [R-1.16]) - builder: a customer can request a return against their own order, and staff review and walk that request through the spec's six-state return lifecycle without any code path moving money

Spec 10.2 line 3470-3474 gives the return its own lifecycle dimension ("Requested, approved, rejected, received, inspected, closed"); spec 4 line 1083 makes the request a customer self-service feature ("Return/refund request where eligible", under the account section's `/account/returns` of line 1045); spec 6.8 line 1949/1957 gives the staff surface (`/admin/returns`, "Return request review") and line 1985 is the rule that governs it ("Never equate 'refund requested' with 'refund completed'"); spec 19.1 lines 4683-4696 name the two customer events ("Return request confirmation", "Return decision"); spec 10.3 lists an allowed source state, destination and actor per transition; spec 1.1 line 92 puts return review in support's mandate; spec 17 line 4285 asks for IDOR tests on the return APIs. The requirement: a customer can request a return against an order, and staff can see and progress that request through its lifecycle.

- **The app choice, taken as instructed and re-checked: `orders/`, beside `Refund`.** A return request is the other half of the money lifecycle `Refund` (orders/models.py) already records, and a new app would have dragged `config/settings.py` + `config/urls.py` registration in for no structural gain. `orders/state.py` was NOT touched: spec 10.2 lists return as a dimension BESIDE fulfilment, so the vocabulary, the transition table and the gate are declared in `orders/models.py` (`ReturnStatus`, `RETURN_ALLOWED_TRANSITIONS`, `return_transition_allowed`) beside the model they describe.

- **ONE model, one new capability pair, and the six spec states verbatim.** `ReturnRequest` carries `order`, `status`, `reason_code` (spec 6.8 line 1959 "Reason codes", a bounded vocabulary rather than free text), `reason_note` (a TextField, so it has no width to enforce) and the two timestamps. Nothing else: no amount column, no refund link, no approval flag.

- **A return request moves NO money, and that is asserted on the persisted row rather than promised in prose.** `MoneyIsUntouchedTests` snapshots `(payment_status, total_amount, refunded_at, refundable_remaining, Refund rows for the order, Refund rows overall)` and compares it after the customer's request, after a staff approval, and after the full walk to `closed`: identical every time, `Refund.objects.count()` still 0, `refundable_remaining` still `1200.00`. Approval is a separate staff act, so nothing auto-approves (spec 6.8 line 1985).

- **Guest returns are NOT supported - a decision, with the spec lines that decide it.** Spec 4 routes returns under the ACCOUNT section (line 1045) and spec line 74 gives the guest "browse products and optionally check out without an account"; returns are never named among the guest's powers. So the seam accepts no guest credential at all, and a guest order (`user is None`) is unreachable through it. `GuestOrderTests` asserts this on the RESPONSE BYTES, not the status code: the customer's own valid B04 token, presented for the exact order that minted it, gets the same one body as an order number that does not exist. The exclusion is structural, not a check that could be forgotten - the lookup filters `user=request.user`, which a NULL-user row can never satisfy.

- **Every LOOKUP miss is ONE byte-identical answer; a malformed body is a 400 and is deliberately not folded into it.** `MissShapeTests.test_every_miss_shape_is_one_shape` builds five shapes in one list, asserts each is refused (400/401/404), then compares `response.content` across the THREE that named an order and reached the lookup - an unknown number, a stranger naming a real order with a REAL JWT, and a guest order with its real token presented - against the baseline's bytes.
