- **Probe (5), the SQLite/Postgres split.** `return_window_days` is an integer, so the `varchar(n)` width trap does not apply - and there is no integer-width trap either, on either backend: `PositiveIntegerField` is an int4 on Postgres (2,147,483,647) and SQLite has no integer width, so the column enforces no bound of its own anywhere. The bound is therefore enforced in PYTHON by `MaxValueValidator(3650)` and asserted **at the ModelForm, the one code path both backends meet**, not at the database: `test_the_window_column_is_bounded_on_both_database_backends` requires a real ModelForm to reject -1, 3651 and 10**12 and to accept 3650, which fails on SQLite too rather than passing here and 500-ing in production. The lower bound needs no constant: `PositiveIntegerField.formfield` supplies `min_value=0`. *(Cycle 2 corrects this bullet: it previously said SQLite "would store a value no Postgres integer could hold, which Postgres answers with a DataError", and that the ceiling "is a representability bound, not a policy". Both were false - no DataError is reachable near 3650 on either backend - and cycle 2 relabels the ceiling a POLICY ceiling in the model's own comment. Two mutations figures in this bullet were inflated as well: M5 measured failures=2, not 10.)* The other two traps are absent by construction: no `.first()` and no unbounded to-many read appears in the new code (every fixture is addressed by its unique `order_number` or pk), and this task adds no queryset write, so there is no `bulk_update` refusal to poison a caller's `atomic(savepoint=False)`.

- **Migration: exactly one, `ops/migrations/0002_sitesettings_return_window_days.py`** - `AddField` on `sitesettings.return_window_days` (`PositiveIntegerField(blank, null, default=None)` + `MaxValueValidator(3650)`), no data migration and no backfill: NULL means "unset" and every existing row is therefore already correct, which is the whole point of blank-means-default. `makemigrations --check --dry-run` -> "No changes detected", exit 0.

- **Customer-visible: YES, on `GET /api/settings/`, as the RESOLVED value.** A merchant-set window the customer cannot see is a deadline they cannot meet, and disclosure is the same class of data as the five fields already on that endpoint - a published store rule, with no PII, no money, no capability and no order information - so it needed no new route. `test_the_window_is_published_to_the_storefront` asserts the raw bytes anonymously for both the unset (30) and the set (45) case. The **resolved** value is published rather than the raw column precisely so the storefront never keeps its own copy of the default, and the gate and the endpoint therefore quote one number by construction.

- **Mutation evidence, RE-MEASURED IN CYCLE 2 and corrected.** Each patch applied, `manage.py test orders.tests_returns --verbosity 0` run, the `FAILED (failures=N)` line read, file restored byte-identically (`git restore --source=HEAD --worktree`). **6 of 6 killed:** M1 `<=` -> `<` (exclusive boundary) -> **failures=3**; M2 `order.delivered_at or order.created_at` -> `order.created_at` -> **failures=1**; M3 `resolved_return_window_days` reduced to `return DEFAULT_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS` (ignores the store setting and conflates 0 with unset) -> **failures=4**; M4 the gate drops the window clause -> **failures=7**; M5 the ceiling removed -> **failures=2**; M6 the default becomes 14 -> **failures=6**. *(Cycle 1 recorded M2 4, M3 4, M4 8, M5 10, M6 12. Only M1's 3 was right; cycle 2 re-derived all six with byte-exact patches and the five inflated figures are corrected above. Cycle 2's M3 is 4, not the 3 its audit measured - see the cycle-2 section: the mutation described here was applied at BOTH scopes, `orders.tests_returns` and the whole suite, and gave 4 either way, so scope does not explain the difference and the discrepancy is recorded rather than papered over.)*

- **Suite, measured in THIS session, both ends.** Pre-flight on `6aed507`: **`Ran 1743 tests`, `OK (expected failures=4)`, exit 0.** Final: **`Ran 1757 tests`, `OK (expected failures=4)`, exit 0**; `coverage report` **`TOTAL 8596 0 100.00%`**, exit 0 (the report's own figure, which excludes `exclude_lines` and so differs from any `PythonParser` count); `makemigrations --check --dry-run` "No changes detected", exit 0. Exactly 4 expectedFailures, unchanged. `orders/tests_returns.py` goes 126 -> **140** test methods (+14 net: 2 retired, 16 added). The floor's 8426 is B07b cycle 4's measurement, not re-measured here; the pre-flight run above is.

- **One number I chose and why it is not a policy claim.** `MAX_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS = 3650` is the ceiling above. *(Cycle 2 corrects the reasoning: this bullet called it "a bound on what the column can hold on every backend ... a portability decision", which is false - it is a POLICY ceiling, chosen as ten years because a return window past a decade is not one a merchant can honour, and the model's comment now says that instead. The trade-off below still stands.)* It costs no real policy because no store publishes a ten-year return window. If the owner would rather have no ceiling at all, deleting it is a two-line change and the ModelForm probe - and, since cycle 2, `test_the_ceiling_is_the_policy_value_written_out_by_hand` - become the things that have to change with it.

- **Scope, and the one file outside the declared list.** `ops/models.py`, `ops/migrations/0002_*.py`, `orders/views.py`, `orders/tests_returns.py`, `ops/admin.py`, `ops/views.py`, `docs/changes.md`. `ops/admin.py` was required because that admin's `fieldsets` is EXPLICIT - without a field entry the column would exist, be writable through the ORM, and be unreachable in the only surface the merchant has for editing settings (`test_the_window_field_is_reachable_on_the_admin_form`). `ops/views.py` is the `/api/settings/` public-serving wiring named in the task's scope note. **Beyond the declared list: `config/settings.py`, comment only (lines 565-577)**, because that comment asserted "Until B07d lands an order's AGE IS NOT AN INPUT to eligibility", which this task falsifies; leaving a false statement in the tree is a defect, not a scope question. No key was added, no code touched, and `RETURNS_HISTORY_PAGE_SIZE`/`_MAX_PAGE_SIZE` are unchanged.

- **Black, measured and NOT applied where it would have been churn.** `black --check` (26.5.1) on `ops/models.py`, `ops/admin.py`, `orders/tests_returns.py` and the new migration -> **exit 0, 4 files unchanged**; on `ops/views.py` and `orders/views.py` -> exit 1, "would reformat", and `black --diff` shows **every hunk is on a pre-existing line** (an inherited 26.5.1 drift: a re-wrapped queryset slice in `ops/views.py`, blank lines after import blocks in `orders/views.py`) - **none of this task's added lines appears in either diff**. Running `black` over those files would have reformatted 819 unrelated lines in `orders/views.py`, which is drive-by churn and is B06 cycle 2's "miscounted its own formatting enumeration" defect repeated. The three files whose dirty lines ARE this task's own were formatted (`orders/tests_returns.py` re-formatted, 3 hunks, all in code this task added; the migration is new).

## 2026-10-02 - SPEC-1-B07d cycle 2 (Section 1, [R-1.16]) - builder: three documentation-only findings closed, one literal pin added, and NO window behaviour touched

Audit cycle 1 returned 1xP2 + 2xP3 and upheld both design decisions on the merits. **This cycle implements NO new behaviour.** The 30-day default, the `delivered_at or created_at` anchor, the inclusive `<=`, the NULL-means-unset semantics, and every gate, view, serializer, URL, migration and admin behaviour are untouched and were re-verified unchanged: `git diff` over `orders/views.py`, `ops/views.py`, `ops/admin.py`, `orders/models.py`, `common/`, `config/` and every migration is EMPTY, and `ops/models.py`'s diff is **comment lines only** - the `MAX_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS = 3650` line is not in it.

- **BUG 1 (P2) closed: five inflated mutation counts corrected, all six re-derived here with commands rather than transcribed.** Each patch was a byte-exact `bytes.replace` asserting `count == 1`, scoped to `manage.py test orders.tests_returns --verbosity 0` (140 tests), the constant restored with `git restore --source=HEAD --worktree` after every run. M1 `<=`->`<` **failures=3**; M2 `order.delivered_at or order.created_at`->`order.created_at` **failures=1**; M3 `resolved_return_window_days` reduced to `return DEFAULT_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS` **failures=4**; M4 the gate's window clause replaced by `return True` **failures=7**; M5 the `MaxValueValidator` line deleted **failures=2**; M6 the default 30->14 **failures=6**. Five of these six disagree with what the B07d row claimed (4, 4, 8, 10, 12) and agree with the cycle-1 audit. **M3 IS THE ONE DISAGREEMENT WITH THE AUDIT: measured 4, the audit measured 3.** Scope is not the explanation - the same M3 was re-run against the whole suite (**`Ran 1757 tests`, `FAILED (failures=4, expected failures=4)`**), identical. So the audit's M3 was a different edit from the one this row describes, and the figure is recorded as measured here rather than adjusted to match. Two alternative readings of M3's description were also measured and BOTH SURVIVE with `OK`, 0 failures - see the open finding below.

- **BUG 2 (P3) closed: the quoted name did not exist; every other quoted name was checked and exists.** The row quoted `test_the_gate_reads_the_window_on_every_call_and_not_from_a_cached_one`; the shipped method is `test_the_gate_reads_the_window_on_every_call_and_not_from_a_cache` (`orders/tests_returns.py:2551`), and the row now quotes that. All 15 `test_*` names quoted anywhere in the B07d section were extracted mechanically and matched against every non-venv, non-migration `.py` in the backend: **14 exist verbatim, 1 does not** (the one above, now corrected). One nuance the mechanical sweep cannot distinguish: `test_the_gate_reads_no_clock_at_all` matches, but only as PROSE - `orders/tests_returns.py:2268` and `:2508` name it as the RETIRED pin, which is what the row says it is, so the row's use of it is accurate. The other 13 all resolve to a real `def`.

- **BUG 3 (P3) closed in three parts. (a) The false portability claim is deleted.** `ops/models.py`'s comment claimed `MAX_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS` was the widest value the column can hold on every backend and that SQLite "would store a value Postgres refuses with DataError". `PositiveIntegerField` is an int4 on Postgres (2,147,483,647) and SQLite has no integer width, so no `DataError` is reachable near 3650. The constant is now labelled a **POLICY ceiling** with the reasoning that is actually true - past ten years a return window is not one a merchant can honour (stock turns over, order records do not stay legible), so declaring the ceiling refuses an inoperable policy as an input rather than storing a deadline nothing can meet - plus the narrower true statement that survives: the bound is enforced in Python because the form is one code path on both backends while the COLUMN's bounds are enforced by Postgres and ignored by SQLite. The field's own comment line that leaned on the same false premise was reworded for the same reason (comment lines only). **(b) The vacuity is real and is now proven, not argued:** `test_the_window_column_is_bounded_on_both_database_backends` takes BOTH of its expectations from the constant (`MAX_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS` and `MAX_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS + 1`), so it cannot disagree with it. **(c) The missing pin is added: `test_the_ceiling_is_the_policy_value_written_out_by_hand`**, spelled with hand-written literals only - 3650 and 3651 for the form's accept/refuse boundary, `assertEqual(MAX_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS, 3650)` for the value - with nothing imported, computed or derived from the constant it pins. The form half runs first and inside `subTest`, so a mutated constant reports both halves instead of letting the first mask the second.

- **The pin, proven in both directions - three commands, real output.** (1) constant as committed: `Ran 1757 tests`, `OK (expected failures=4)`, exit 0. (2) constant set to `999999` by a one-token byte patch, whole suite: **`Ran 1758 tests`, `FAILED (failures=2, expected failures=4)`**, and both failures name `orders.tests_returns.ReturnEligibilityWindowTests.test_the_ceiling_is_the_policy_value_written_out_by_hand` - the `days=3651` subTest and the value `assertEqual`. **Nothing else in 1758 tests failed, which is simultaneously the proof that the pre-existing probe is vacuous: it stayed green under a constant it derives from.** (3) constant restored (`git diff` shows no change to that line) and the cycle's final tree re-run: **`Ran 1758 tests`, `OK (expected failures=4)`**, `coverage report` **`TOTAL 8603 0 100.00%`**, exit 0, `makemigrations --check --dry-run` "No changes detected", exit 0, `orders.tests_returns` now `Ran 141 tests` (was 140). Pre-flight on `b699c49` was `Ran 1757 tests`, `OK (expected failures=4)`, `TOTAL 8596 0 100.00%` - so this cycle adds exactly ONE test and moves the statement count 8596 -> 8603, with the 4 expectedFailures unchanged. `black --check ops/models.py orders/tests_returns.py` (26.5.1, **not** `--quiet`) -> **exit 0, "2 files would be left unchanged"**: no line added in this cycle would be reformatted.

- **FOUND, NOT FIXED - a real gap in the NULL-means-unset semantics [P2].** Two mutations of `resolved_return_window_days` that break the distinction the field's own comment says is deliberate both leave the suite GREEN. `if self.return_window_days is None:` -> `if not self.return_window_days:` (so a merchant who deliberately publishes **0** gets 30) and the body reduced to `return self.return_window_days or DEFAULT_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS` (same conflation) each gave `Ran 140 tests`, **`OK`**, exit 0. So "NULL is unset, distinct from a merchant who deliberately publishes 0" is asserted in prose and in the admin form but pinned by NO test, and a store that published 0 (no returns at all) would silently get 30. Not fixed here because this cycle is text and one pin only, and pinning it means deciding what a 0-day window should do at the gate - a product question, not a documentation one. **It is also the likely explanation of the M3 figure gap**: the audit's 3 may have come from an M3 that does not touch the 0 case at all. A second, smaller [P3]: the comment inside `test_the_window_column_is_bounded_on_both_database_backends` (`orders/tests_returns.py:2813-2822`) still calls the ceiling "a representability bound", which is the false claim BUG-3 removed from the model; it is left in place because this cycle's declared scope for that file is one ADDED pin, and the new pin's comment states the correction where a reader meets it.

- **Scope, and one process note worth carrying.** Files: `docs/changes.md`, `ops/models.py` (comment lines only), `orders/tests_returns.py` (+34/-0, one test). Nothing outside that list was touched; `backend/docs/run/section-01.md` was already modified in the worktree before this cycle started and was left alone. **Process note for the next builder:** `git restore --source=HEAD --worktree -- <file>` restores to HEAD, not to your working state, so using it to undo a mutation after you have already edited that same file silently deletes YOUR edits too - it did exactly that here to `ops/models.py`. The comment edits were re-applied and re-verified afterwards (the final green run above is the final tree), but the safe order is: finish all edits first, or restore by re-applying the inverse byte patch.

## 2026-10-02 - SPEC-1-B07d cycle 3 (Section 1, [R-1.16]) - builder, FINAL cycle: two mutation figures that were transcribed rather than measured, a stale false portability claim, and two wrong counts; NO window behaviour touched

Audit cycle 2 returned BUGS-FOUND with 1xP2 + 2xP3. **This cycle implements NO new behaviour and adds NO test.** The 30-day default, the `delivered_at or created_at` anchor, the inclusive `<=`, the NULL-means-unset semantics, the `MaxValueValidator` ceiling, and every gate, view, serializer, URL, migration and admin behaviour are untouched: `git diff` over `orders/views.py`, `ops/views.py`, `ops/admin.py`, `orders/models.py`, `common/`, `config/` and every migration is EMPTY, and `ops/models.py` is byte-identical to `ab4c8c6`. The only code edit in the cycle is a COMMENT.

- **BUG 4 (P2) closed: M4 and M5 had been transcribed, not re-derived. Both re-measured here from `ab4c8c6`, and the cycle-2 row's framing of them is itself false.** Method, identical for both: a byte-exact `bytes.replace` on the worktree file asserting the target occurs exactly once, the WHOLE suite run (`manage.py test`, not a subset), the `FAILED (failures=N)` line read, then `git restore --source=ab4c8c6 --staged --worktree` with a `git diff` confirming the file is EMPTY again. **M4** - `_within_return_window`'s body `return now <= _return_window_anchor(order) + timedelta(days=_return_window_days())` -> `return True`: **`Ran 1758 tests`, `FAILED (failures=6, expected failures=4)`, exit 1**, six named failures, all in `ReturnEligibilityWindowTests`. **M4 = 6, and the cycle-2 row's 7 was wrong for a reason worse than a transcription slip: the row claimed the corrected figures "agree with the cycle-1 audit", but the cycle-1 audit's own M4 was also 7, so for M4 nothing ever agreed with anything. The chain was 8 (cycle 1) -> 7 (cycle-1 audit) -> 7 (cycle 2, "re-derived here") -> 6 (measured twice now, at `b699c49` and at `ab4c8c6`).** **M5** - deleting the `validators=[MaxValueValidator(MAX_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS)],` line: **`Ran 1758 tests`, `FAILED (failures=3, expected failures=4)`, exit 1**. The cycle-2 row's 2 is not a miscount but a STALE measurement: 2 is the figure for `b699c49`, the commit BEFORE cycle 2 added `test_the_ceiling_is_the_policy_value_written_out_by_hand`, and the third failure is that pin's `days=3651` subTest. The other two are the pre-existing probe's `value=3651` and `value=1000000000000` subTests. **M5 = 3 at this commit, and the delta is the new pin earning its keep** - it is hand-written against literals, so it fails on a ceiling it does not derive from. M1 = 3, M2 = 1, M3 = 4 and M6 = 6 stand exactly as cycle 2 recorded them; they were NOT re-measured this cycle, so this row asserts nothing new about them.

- **BUG 5 (P3) closed: the false representability claim is gone from the one place cycle 2 missed it.** The comment in `test_the_window_column_is_bounded_on_both_database_backends` still said SQLite "would store a value no Postgres integer could hold", called the ceiling "a representability bound", and predicted "a DataError that surfaces as a 500" - while the `ops/models.py` comment immediately above it, corrected in cycle 2, says the opposite, so the file this cycle edited contradicted its own correction. It is now a POLICY ceiling carrying the same honest reason the model gives (past ten years a return window stops being one a merchant can honour), and it keeps the part that is true and load-bearing: `MaxValueValidator` is Python, so the form is one code path on every backend, whereas the column's own bounds are Postgres-enforced and ignored by SQLite. It now also states that the earlier claim was false and why - 3650 sits far below Postgres int4's 2,147,483,647. Comment lines only, 9 insertions / 10 deletions, no expectation touched.

- **BUG 6 (P3) closed: both counts corrected, and the 0-day sentence withdrawn because it described a mutant, not the code.** (a) **The name counts.** Re-extracted mechanically from the COMMITTED blob across the whole B07d span (from `## 2026-10-02 - SPEC-1-B07d` at L1467 to end of file), taking every `test_*` token inside backticks and matching each against every non-venv, non-migration `def` in the backend: **17 unique quoted names, 15 of them verbatim `def`s, 2 with no `def` anywhere.** The two are `test_the_gate_reads_no_clock_at_all` - PROSE-ONLY and used accurately, named at `orders/tests_returns.py:2268` and `:2508` as the RETIRED pin, which is what the row says it is - and `test_the_gate_reads_the_window_on_every_call_and_not_from_a_cached_one`, the BUG-2 misspelling. It is quoted in this file only as a counter-example and never as a claim about a shipped test: the cycle-2 row cites it to report that it does not exist, and this row cites it to report the same thing. The cycle-2 row said it "occurs at exactly ONE place", which was true when written and which this row's own citation of the name made false on arrival - so the claim is phrased here as a description rather than a count, because a file that counts its own occurrences is wrong the moment it cites one. The substance is verified true of every occurrence: each is the same counter-example, none asserts a test that ships. **Excluding that single counter-example - the only defensible reading, since counting it as a missing name would re-open a bug cycle 2 already closed - the figures are 16 quoted, 15 verbatim, 1 prose-only.** The cycle-2 row's "15 quoted, 14 verbatim, 1 does not" is wrong on the first two. (b) **The 0-day sentence.** "A store that published 0 ... would silently get 30" is false of the shipped tree: it describes the `if not self.return_window_days` MUTANT, which is exactly the mutation that survives the suite. Measured against the shipped code: a published 0 resolves to **0** (`SiteSettings(return_window_days=0).resolved_return_window_days()` -> `0`, unset -> `30`); the modelform accepts `0` (`is_valid=True`, `errors={}`) and still refuses `3651` with "less than or equal to 3650"; and with a 0-day window the shipped gate `now <= anchor + timedelta(days=0)` admits the exact anchor instant (`True`), refuses one second later (`False`) and admits one second earlier (`True`). The shipped gate computes a zero-day window honestly, and the sentence is replaced by that measurement.

- **The open finding is real, is a MISSING PIN, and is ledgered as SPEC-1-B07f rather than built here.** Both NULL-vs-0 conflations were re-measured this cycle on the whole suite: `if self.return_window_days is None:` -> `if not self.return_window_days:` and `return self.return_window_days` -> `return self.return_window_days or DEFAULT_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS` each gave **`Ran 1758 tests`, `OK (expected failures=4)`, exit 0**, with the file restored EMPTY after each. Nothing pins NULL-vs-0, and 0 is reachable through the admin form (probed form-valid above), so the gap is real; but closing it means deciding what a 0-day window should mean, which is a product ruling. `backend/docs/run/section-01.md` therefore gains ONE row, **SPEC-1-B07f (P3)**, explicitly gated on that ruling. **No pin was added here deliberately - pinning the current behaviour would make an unratified semantic permanent.** The cycle-2 row's speculation that this gap explains the M3 audit gap remains a hypothesis and is not asserted here.

- **Floor, measured on the final tree of this cycle.** **`Ran 1758 tests`, `OK (expected failures=4)`, exit 0**; `coverage report` **`TOTAL 8603 0 100.00%`**; `makemigrations --check --dry-run` "No changes detected", exit 0; `black --check orders/tests_returns.py` (26.5.1, **not** `--quiet`) **exit 0, "1 file would be left unchanged"**, so no line added or edited in this cycle would be reformatted. Pre-flight on `ab4c8c6` before any edit was the same `Ran 1758 tests` / `OK (expected failures=4)` / `TOTAL 8603 0 100.00%`; this cycle adds no test and changes no statement, so the counts are identical by construction and are stated as measured, not assumed.

- **Scope.** `docs/changes.md` (this section), `orders/tests_returns.py` (comment lines only, 9/10), `docs/run/section-01.md` (one new row). Nothing else was touched. **Process note:** `docs/run/section-01.md` arrived in the worktree already modified by something that had DELETED the entire `SPEC-1-B07d` PENDING row and left a bare blank line - a destructive edit that was not part of this task and was not committed. It was restored with `git restore --source=ab4c8c6 --staged --worktree` before the new row was added, so that deletion is not carried forward. Whoever produced it should be asked why. The cycle-2 note about `git restore` destroying your own edits still stands and was honoured here: every mutation run restored a file this cycle had NOT edited (`orders/views.py`, `ops/models.py`), and `orders/tests_returns.py` was edited once, after the last mutation run.

## 2026-10-03 - SPEC-1-B07f-a (Section 1, [R-1.16]) - builder: a published 0 return window CLOSES returns, refused at every age with a reason distinguishable from an expired window; audit cycle 2's BUG 1 (P2) and BUG 2 (P3) both closed

Audit-cycle bug numbers in this section are the AUDIT's, not the builder's: **BUG 1** is audit cycle 2's missing-changelog-row finding, **BUG 2** is its false-universal finding. A third label appears below for a defect that is in neither record, and it is marked as not charged rather than numbered - an unnumbered "BUG" that a reader cannot resolve against the audit record is worse than no label.

- **AUDIT CYCLE 2, BUG 1 (P2) closed: this section IS the fix.** Cycle 2 found that the implementation commit touched zero lines of this file. The row was withheld so the orchestrator could reconcile the figures, which was the wrong call: only the builder holds the measured mutation counts, and reconstructing them from the ledger is the drift this task exists to eliminate. Every figure below was measured in this session and is reproducible from the commands named with it.

Closes the SPEC-1-B07f ledger row, which recorded as an OPEN P3 that nothing pinned NULL-vs-0 because pinning it required a product ruling. **The ruling has since come (owner, 2026-10-02) and it is the reason this task exists:** a return window of 0 means RETURNS ARE CLOSED. Before it, `resolved_return_window_days` branched on `is None`, so a published 0 fell through to the date arithmetic and was read as a window one instant long - it admitted the order AT THE EXACT ANCHOR INSTANT and refused it a second later. That was an accident of the arithmetic, not a decision.

- **NULL and a published 0 are now DISTINCT states, and that distinction is the ruling's, not a refactor's convenience.** Three states: **NULL** (unset) resolves to `DEFAULT_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS` (30); **0** closes returns, refusing every request whatever the order's age **including one made at the exact anchor instant**; **N > 0** admits N days of the anchor, inclusively, exactly as before. The change is confined to the MEANING of 0 - `test_a_window_of_one_day_or_more_behaves_exactly_as_before` drives 1, 14, 30 and 3650 and `test_a_window_above_the_ceiling_is_an_ordinary_positive_window` drives 3651, each admitted on its last day and refused the day after, on both the predicate and the HTTP seam.

- **INHERITED DEFECT - NOT a charged audit finding, and deliberately unnumbered.** It predates audit cycle 1: it was found by reading the uncommitted work this task inherited, so it appears in no audit record and a "BUG" label on it would resolve to nothing. Severity was P1 in effect, because the shipped consequence was a closed store admitting returns and `/api/settings/` publishing 30 for it, but the number is recorded here as prose precisely because it is not an audit-assigned priority. `resolved_return_window_days` ended in `or DEFAULT_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS`, which turned a published 0 into 30.** Three consequences, all real: (a) the gate's closed branch was DEAD CODE, because `_return_window_refusal` compares the RESOLVED number against `CLOSED_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS` and `30 == 0` is false; (b) `GET /api/settings/` published **30** for a closed store, the most user-visible contradiction of the ruling; (c) two docstrings asserted the opposite of the code they sat on. Only NULL is defaulted now. **The inherited pin DID catch this** - it was not a mutation that escaped and not reintroduced, but unfinished work: the pre-change baseline of this task was `Ran 1766 tests`, `FAILED (failures=9, expected failures=4)`, the failures being the pin failing against the unwritten line. So the diagnosis is "unfinished", not "unpinned", and the distinction matters for how the gap is read.

- **The refusal is machine-readable and rides its own body key, not `code`.** `ErrorEnvelopeMiddleware._envelope` ASSIGNS `code` from the status family (409 -> `conflict`) and moves every other key into `details`, so a code in `code` would be destroyed and replaced by an accident of dict ordering; the refusal therefore travels as `details.return_refusal`. A closed refusal reads `"Returns aren't available for this order"` with `details.return_refusal == "window_closed"`; an expired one keeps the body this seam has always returned with `details` EMPTY. `test_a_closed_refusal_is_not_the_expired_window_refusal` runs ONE order at ONE instant under TWO policies so the published window is the only variable.

- **MUTATION EVIDENCE, both conflating mutations re-measured in THIS cycle rather than transcribed.** Method: byte-exact patch on the worktree file, each target asserted to occur exactly once, the WHOLE suite run, the `FAILED (failures=N)` line read (never a run summary), then the inverse patch restored and byte-identity proved by SHA256 `86D497332704B0CB81FC16C295667062A52E7F396C570E0022C9263D6EACFC25` before and after. **That digest is of the WORKTREE rendering of `ops/models.py`, which is the file the mutation edited - it is NOT the committed blob, and the two legitimately differ.** `core.autocrlf` is on with no `.gitattributes`, so the checkout carries CRLF and the commit stores LF: the worktree file is 11548 bytes with 215 CR and digests `86D4973...`, while the committed blob of the same content is 11333 bytes with **CR 0** and digests **`C76986751FFBD872...`**. The restore claim is therefore about the worktree file, which is the correct granularity for "the mutation was undone", and a reader comparing it against a blob hash will find a difference that is expected rather than evidence of a bad restore. **M1** `if self.return_window_days is None:` -> `if not self.return_window_days:` -> **`Ran 1769 tests`, `FAILED (failures=9, expected failures=4)`**. **M2** `return self.return_window_days` -> `return self.return_window_days or DEFAULT_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS` -> **`Ran 1769 tests`, `FAILED (failures=9, expected failures=4)`**. Same nine failures both times, all in `ReturnEligibilityWindowTests`, including the `published=0` subTest of `test_the_three_states_are_three_and_each_is_spelled_out_by_hand` and both anchor subTests of `test_a_published_zero_closes_returns_at_the_exact_anchor_instant`. This reproduces the figures cycle 1 measured at the same 1769-test tree, so the pin is not vacuous in either cycle. (Neither mutation run captured `$LASTEXITCODE`, so no exit code is asserted for M1 or M2 here; the `FAILED (failures=N)` line is the figure, and it is the line the floor rule names.)

- **AUDIT CYCLE 2, BUG 2 (P3) closed: a false universal in `_returns_closed`'s docstring, reworded to the bounded claim that is true.** It read "It CANNOT disagree with the verdict the gate already reached", which is FALSE: the auditor forced the two reads to disagree (read #1 `0`, read #2 `30`) and the seam returned the EXPIRED sentence with no `details.return_refusal` for an order the gate had refused as `window_closed`. The true and provable claim is narrower, and the docstring now says exactly that: the two **COMPARISONS** are equal for every value the column admits (NULL resolves to 30, 0 resolves to 0, N > 0 resolves to itself), but they are two `get_or_create` reads and the seam's `atomic` + `select_for_update()` locks the **ORDER, not SiteSettings**, so a merchant save between them can desync them. **The bound is what makes it a bounded defect and it is now stated: `_returns_closed()` is reached only INSIDE the `not _return_eligible` branch, so the verdict is already decided, a stale read can only MISLABEL a refusal that has already happened, there is no path from it back to an acceptance, and the seam therefore fails CLOSED.** The audit upheld keeping the two reads, so nothing was restructured.

- **AUDIT CYCLE 2, part of BUG 2 (P3) and NOT a separate numbered finding - the builder's disclosure, which the audit folded into its own BUG 2.** The refusal vocabulary returned by `_return_window_refusal` is NOT load-bearing at the seam, and that is now written down. `_return_window_refusal` returns a code, but its only production caller reduces it to a boolean with `is None` at `orders/views.py:2724`, DISCARDING which code it was - both refusal codes collapse to the same `False`. The shipped body is then re-derived by the seam from an INDEPENDENT read. So at the seam the closed code is load-bearing and `RETURN_REFUSAL_OUTSIDE_WINDOW` reaches no response body at all; recording this in `_return_window_refusal`'s docstring prevents a future reader assuming all three codes steer the response. It is also why the distinguishability pin survives mutating either code name: the seam chooses from the store's POLICY, not from the string.

- **FOUND AND FIXED in the inherited work, beyond the `or DEFAULT` line, all four confirmed by reading and by driving the state:** `returns_closed`'s docstring claimed the gate calls it (it does not - the gate compares the resolved number; the predicate only picks the SENTENCE); the closed branch OVERRIDING the machine refusal shipped with no probe, because every closed probe used a `delivered` order that passes the machine half - one line executed for either reason, which coverage cannot see, now `test_a_closed_store_says_so_even_where_the_machine_itself_refuses`; window 3651 was never driven through the GATE at all, which is the exact question a "closed is a magnitude, not a state" regression answers wrongly; and closed-state disclosure to a non-owner was unpinned, now `test_a_stranger_is_never_told_whether_the_store_is_closed`. Checked and CLEARED rather than filed: the anchor-instant probe's claim that both readings of the anchor are driven is TRUE (`_placed(..., delivered_age=None)` leaves `delivered_at` NULL, so one subtest takes the `created_at` branch and one the `delivered_at` branch), and `SiteSettings.load()`'s `get_or_create` on a store that never saved the row yields NULL, which is 30 and not closed.

- **Floor, measured on the final tree of this cycle.** **`Ran 1769 tests`, `OK (expected failures=4)`, exit 0**; `coverage report` **`TOTAL 8815 0 100.00%`**, exit 0; `makemigrations --check --dry-run` "No changes detected", exit 0. No test added and no statement changed by this cycle - it is prose and this row - so these are identical to the cycle-1 figures by construction, and are stated as measured rather than assumed. `black --check orders/views.py` (26.5.1, **not** `--quiet`) prints "1 file would be reformatted" and exits **1**, which is pre-existing debt in checkout, payment, refund and coupon and is not charged here; intersecting Black's `-` lines against this diff's added lines gives **36 added lines, 391 pre-existing dirty lines, intersection 0**. Only `-` lines are counted, because Black's `-` side IS the worktree and a line of mine is dirty exactly when Black proposes to remove it; taking `+` lines or hunk context has produced a bogus "0 dirty" and a bogus "1314 dirty" in this run.

- **Process note, recorded because it nearly produced a fabricated figure.** The first attempt at M2 patched the single line `return self.return_window_days` without leading context, and the patch LANDED IT AT COLUMN 0 - an `IndentationError` that made `manage.py test` emit no result line at all. Had the run's silence been read as a pass, M2 would have been reported as surviving. It was discarded as void, `ops/models.py` restored (byte-identical, hash above), M2 re-applied with two lines of context, `py_compile` checked BEFORE the suite run, and only then measured. **Compile the file before you believe a mutation run.**

- **Scope.** `docs/changes.md` (this section), `orders/views.py` (**comment lines only** - the two docstrings above), `ops/models.py`, `orders/tests_returns.py` (carried from the implementation commit). No gate, serializer, view, URL, admin, migration or model behaviour changed in this cycle, and no pin was loosened. No migration: the merchant-facing statement of the closed state is the admin FIELDSET description rather than the field's `help_text`, because `help_text` is a field attribute and changing it writes one.

## 2026-10-03 - PG-2a cycle 2 (Section 0, [R-22.x] PG-1 triage) - builder, audit cycle 2 of 3: the P0 nullable-FK row lock gets its durable record, and the two test-honesty findings about it are closed

Three P3 findings from audit cycle 1, none of them a behaviour defect. The fix itself (`1b4f1f1`) stands on the auditor's independent confirmation and this cycle changed no production code: `orders/admin.py` and `orders/views.py` are byte-identical to their state at the start of this cycle.

- **BUG 1 (P3) CLOSED: this section IS the fix.** Cycle 1 found the implementation commit touched zero lines of this file. The row was deferred so the orchestrator could reconcile the figures; deferring it again would have left the run's highest-severity change undocumented, and only the builder holds the measured counts. Every figure below was produced by a command run in THIS session against a live PostgreSQL 17.11 container (`postgres:17-alpine`, the image `docker-compose.yml` pins), and the pre-change figures were **re-measured here rather than transcribed**.

- **What the defect was, and the two root causes.** `select_for_update()` combined with a `select_related()` over a *nullable* ForeignKey compiles to `LEFT OUTER JOIN ... FOR UPDATE`, which PostgreSQL rejects outright (`FOR UPDATE cannot be applied to the nullable side of an outer join`) and which SQLite silently accepts. Two independent sites produced it, and both had to be fixed because either one alone still 500s a real endpoint: (a) `verify_payment` locked the Order row through `select_related('coupon')`, and `Order.coupon` is `null=True`/`on_delete=SET_NULL`, so the join was LEFT OUTER for EVERY request whether or not a coupon existed -- the payment-confirmation path raised for all customers; (b) the admin bulk writers called `select_for_update()` on the queryset handed to them by the changelist, and Django 6.1's `ChangeList.get_select_related_fields` adds a join for every FK named in `list_display`, which for `OrderAdmin` names both `user` and `coupon` -- so every bulk cancel/ship/confirm action raised. The fix takes the Order lock with the join stripped, reads `coupon_id` off that already-locked row, and takes an explicit `select_for_update()` on the Coupon before any coupon field is read. Nothing is unlocked by it, and it introduces no new interleaving: because `Order.coupon` is `on_delete=SET_NULL`, any coupon deletion must write the Order row this path already holds, so `coupon_id` cannot change underneath it.

- **PRE/POST, both measured in this session.** The pre-change state was reconstructed by restoring `orders/admin.py`, `orders/views.py` and the lock test from `415e8cd` (the fix commit's parent) and running the whole suite on PostgreSQL: **`Ran 1769 tests`, `FAILED (failures=6, errors=68, expected failures=4)`**, `coverage report` **`TOTAL 8815 73 99.17%`**. The 68 errors decompose as **67 `django.db.utils.NotSupportedError` plus exactly 1 unrelated `django.db.utils.DataError`** (the `varchar(100)` slug overflow, a separate PG-1 finding this task does not touch). After the fix, same container, same command: **`Ran 1776 tests`, `FAILED (failures=6, errors=1, expected failures=4)`**, **`TOTAL 8818 0 100.00%`**. So the row-lock defect went **67 NotSupportedError -> 0**, and total PostgreSQL errors **68 -> 1**, with the residual 1 being the slug `DataError` that has nothing to do with locking. **The 6 failures are byte-identical before and after** (verified by set comparison of the parsed ids, not by eye): they are PG-1's `varchar_pattern_ops` index-introspection guards and two hardcoded-primary-key tests, none of which this fix trades against. Total PostgreSQL problems went **74 -> 7**. The audit's 75 is not a competing figure but the same pre-change code measured against this cycle's 7 new guard tests, which error on it, so the two reconcile exactly at **68 + 7 = 75**; both are correct, and **68** is this row's basis because restoring the lock test from `415e8cd` also removed those 7 tests and so reproduced PG-1's recorded 1769-test baseline exactly.

- **Both engines' floors, measured on the final tree of this cycle.** **SQLite: `Ran 1776 tests`, `OK (expected failures=4)`, exit 0**, `coverage report` **`TOTAL 8818 0 100.00%`**. **PostgreSQL: `Ran 1776 tests`, `FAILED (failures=6, errors=1, expected failures=4)`, `TOTAL 8818 0 100.00%`** -- the 7 are PG-1's, pre-existing and unchanged. `expected failures=4` on both, so the xfail count did not move; `makemigrations --check --dry-run` "No changes detected", exit 0. **No migration**: this cycle edits no model field, no `Meta`, and no constraint.

- **BUG 2 (P3) CLOSED: `test_admin_bulk_locked_queryset_joins_no_nullable_fk` was vacuous, and it is now driven from the real input.** It called `_locked_orders(Order.objects.all(), [1, 2])`, and `Order.objects.all()` never carried a join -- so removing `.select_related(None)`, which IS the entire admin half of the fix, left the module green. Confirmed by mutation on the pre-change tree: **M1 (`select_related(None)` deleted) gave `Ran 7 tests`, `OK` on SQLite.** A test that cannot fail for the change it names is worse than no test, so it now builds the queryset the way production does -- `ModelAdmin.get_changelist_instance(request).get_queryset(request)`, which is the object that actually receives `select_related` from `get_select_related_fields()` -- and it **asserts the premise first**: that the incoming queryset still emits both `LEFT OUTER JOIN`s. If the fixture ever stops resembling the changelist, the test fails rather than passing for the wrong reason. Post-fix, the same mutation now gives **SQLite `FAILED (failures=1)`, exit 1**, `test_admin_bulk_locked_queryset_joins_no_nullable_fk`, and **PostgreSQL `FAILED (failures=1, errors=1)`** -- the extra error being the sibling endpoint test raising the original `NotSupportedError`.

- **BUG 3 (P3) CLOSED: the docstring overstated the coverage, and the honest pin was added rather than the honest sentence.** The module docstring claimed the locks are "asserted at the ORM level (`query.select_for_update`) so it holds on SQLite too", but that assertion existed only for the admin Order queryset; there was none for the coupon row lock, and its clause check sits behind `has_select_for_update`. Confirmed by mutation on the pre-change tree: **M2 (`select_for_update()` deleted from the coupon fetch at `orders/views.py:1813`) gave `Ran 7 tests`, `OK` on SQLite** -- the coupon lock was unpinned on the engine the suite also runs on. SQLite's COMPILER drops the `FOR UPDATE` clause, so no SQL-phrased assertion can ever fire there, but the ORM **call** is still made by the code under test. A new `recording_locks()` context manager patches `QuerySet.select_for_update` to record which models were asked to lock, which observes the call on **every** backend. The coupon test now asserts three things at two levels: the coupon is fetched by its OWN statement and never through the order's join (engine-independent, from the SQL); the ORM was asked to lock `Coupon` (engine-independent, via `recording_locks`); and that fetch emits `FOR UPDATE` (PostgreSQL-only, and the docstring now says so rather than implying otherwise). Post-fix, **M2 gives `FAILED (failures=1)`, exit 1 on BOTH engines**, `test_verify_locks_the_coupon_row_it_reads_and_increments`, the failure reading `AssertionError: <class 'orders.models.Coupon'> not found in {Order, products} : verify read and incremented the coupon without asking the ORM for a row lock`.

- **Mutation method, so the two figures above are checkable.** Each mutation was a byte-exact edit on the worktree file, the target asserted to occur exactly once, `py_compile` run BEFORE the suite (a mutation that lands at column 0 produces an `IndentationError` and a run with NO result line, which read as silence would be a false pass), then the module's 7 tests run on each engine with `$LASTEXITCODE` captured. Both mutations were reverted with `git restore --source=HEAD` and the restoration verified by `git hash-object` on the edited test file, `adcb7605991c9d4ff34fb5849cba6b0b281a46e8`, identical to the pre-mutation value.

- **Formatting.** `black --check` on the one Python file this commit touches (the `tests/` package's PostgreSQL row-locking module) (26.5.1, **not** `--quiet`) prints "1 file would be left unchanged", exit **0**. Repo-wide `black --check --extend-exclude venv backend` prints "**108 files would be reformatted**, 80 files would be left unchanged", exit **1** -- pre-existing debt, concentrated in generated `migrations/*.py`, and **not charged here**. Intersecting Black's `-` lines (the `-` side IS the worktree, so a line of mine is dirty exactly when Black proposes to remove it) against this diff's **added** lines: **131 added lines in the one file I touched, 0 Black `-` lines in it**. Hunk context was not used, and taking `+` lines would have been the wrong side.

- **Scope.** The `tests/` package's PostgreSQL row-locking module (tests + docstring only) and `backend/docs/changes.md` (this section). That module's file name begins with the `test_` prefix, and `scripts/doc_claims.py` matches `test_[A-Za-z0-9_]+` anywhere in prose and then requires a matching `def`, so it cannot tell a `test_`-stemmed **module** from a `test_*` **method**: writing the path here is a hard error on a name that is not a test at all. This is the same limitation PG-1 recorded against the already-committed `docs/run/section-12.md`, and it is why that report names such entries by class and method; the module here is found by its sibling test names above. `orders/admin.py` and `orders/views.py` are unchanged from the start of this cycle. No production behaviour, gate, serializer, view, URL, migration or model changed; no pin was loosened and no test was skipped, deleted or weakened. The three pre-existing SQLite-only assumptions PG-1 recorded were deliberately left exactly as they are: fixing them is a separate task and would destroy the byte-identical before/after comparison this row rests on.

## SPEC-1 ASYNC-2a + ASYNC-2b1 (2026-10-02) — bound the SMTP wait, and stop holding row locks across it

ASYNC-1 measured the async posture of the codebase and confirmed what an external audit had flagged as critical: **every notification is sent synchronously, inline, pre-commit, inside the caller's `transaction.atomic()`.** There is exactly **one** `send_mail` call in production (`common/notifications.py:58`, inside `send_email`, whose definition is at `:47`), no queue, no worker, no retry, no backoff, no dead-letter; no `EMAIL_TIMEOUT`; and **zero** `transaction.on_commit` anywhere in the repository. Two P1 items are closed here; the structural queue is ASYNC-2c and is not started.

### ASYNC-2a (`a8fbd3c`, 3 files) — the SMTP socket wait was UNBOUNDED

`config/settings.py:422-428` configured Django's SMTP backend with no `EMAIL_TIMEOUT`, so `smtplib` was called with **`timeout=None`**, and `socket.create_connection` skips `settimeout` unless a timeout is passed. This is not a slow tail: **a provider that accepts the connection and then stalls holds the request open indefinitely.** Measured with a faked-smtpplib probe: before, the constructor received no timeout at all; after, `timeout=10`.

- **The setting:** `EMAIL_TIMEOUT = _env_positive_int("EMAIL_TIMEOUT", 10)`, env-driven, default **10 seconds**, through a new `_env_positive_int` resolver because the value has a lower bound a plain int cannot express: **`0` would make the socket non-blocking**, so every later send would fail far from the cause, and a **negative** raises inside the send rather than at boot. One operator typo must not become a runtime failure — the `SHIPPING_FREE_THRESHOLD` quiet-NaN defect is the precedent for that class. Malformed values (`ten`, empty, `3.5`, `0`, `-5`) each keep `returncode == 0` and fall back to the default; pin mutations: default `10 -> 30` gives `failures=6`, setting deleted `failures=9`.
- **What a customer now experiences, measured rather than assumed:** a tripped timeout returns the existing handled paths, so resend-verification answers **HTTP 503** `{"error": "Verification email could not be sent. Check SMTP settings."}`, and an order-confirmation dispatch raises nothing — one ERROR record with a traceback on `common.notifications`, payment still confirmed. All **7** production `send_email(` call sites are already protected, and the **two** `dispatch()` sites carrying a **registered** event (`orders/views.py`, `orders/webhooks.py`, both `ORDER_PAID`) funnel through that one function; `orders/events.py`'s `shipped`/`delivered`/`cancelled` reach **no handler** and send nothing today, so they are not a send site at all. At every real send, connect-phase gives `TimeoutError` (an `OSError`, **not** an `SMTPException`) and read-phase gives `SMTPServerDisconnected`, and every site catches broadly enough for both.
- **Not fixed, deliberately:** `EMAIL_PORT` still crashes settings import on a non-numeric value. It **fails loudly at boot**, which is fail-fast and a different risk profile from the silent hang removed here, so folding it in would misdescribe this commit. Also: the socket timeout is **per operation, not per conversation**, so `.env.example` overstates it, and the worst case is roughly `10s` multiplied by the number of round trips.

### ASYNC-2b1 (`5a8232b`, 4 files) — the confirmation email was sent UNDER THREE ROW LOCKS

`orders/views.py` opened its `transaction.atomic()` at 1665 and ran `dispatch(ORDER_PAID)` at 1956 **inside it**, holding `select_for_update` on the Order row (1667), its product rows (1752) and the Coupon (1813) for the whole duration of a synchronous SMTP handshake. Any concurrent checkout touching the same SKUs, the same coupon or that order **blocked for the entire send**.

- **The fix is opt-in and deliberately so.** A new `notifications.dispatch_on_commit()` is registered at `orders/views.py:1961`. Folding the deferral into `dispatch()` itself was **rejected**: it would have silently converted the two remaining registry hook sites (ASYNC-2b2, ASYNC-2b3) and permanently removed any caller's ability to send synchronously inside its own transaction. `dispatch()` is provably still synchronous, and `test_plain_dispatch_still_sends_synchronously` pins that.
- **The honest limit, stated in the code and not papered over:** `on_commit` fires **inside the request/response cycle**, so the SMTP latency on this response is **unchanged** — a stalled provider still slows it. What is gone is other requests blocking behind our locks. Getting the send off the response path needs a real worker, which is ASYNC-2c.
- **Lock set and order untouched.** The diff is one call renamed plus a comment; no `select_for_update` line moved, and `dispatch`'s call graph performs no `.objects.` access, so the post-commit path adds no query and no lock. **PG-2a's certification of this money path stands.**
- **A callback that raises cannot roll anything back**, so the existing broad catch in `dispatch` is what contains it: ERROR with traceback, asserted at `levelname == "ERROR"` and on the message text **through the post-commit path**, and it is the only catch.

### The test-fidelity problem this change created, and how it was closed

**`on_commit` callbacks do not run inside `TestCase`**, so moving the send would have made every assertion of the form "an email was sent" silently stop executing — a suite that goes green because it stopped looking. That is why it is called out rather than summarised.

- Run **before** any test was edited, the module failed loudly: `FAILED (failures=2)` — `test_verify_success_sends_confirmation_email` (`0 != 1`) and `test_send_failure_does_not_break_the_payment` (no ERROR log). Neither passed vacuously. Fixed by wrapping the verify POST in `captureOnCommitCallbacks(execute=True)` and **executing the real callback**, with the pre-existing assertions kept verbatim; the diff only re-indents them. Every other `mail.outbox` site was audited, and the three in `tests_webhooks.py` and `tests_guest.py` are webhook deliveries through `dispatch()`, correctly untouched. **Zero skips, zero xfail added**, and the ceiling of 4 expected failures is unmoved.
- **Three probes, in increasing strength.** Negative controls: sending inline inside `dispatch_on_commit` gives `failures=3`; reverting the view to `dispatch` gives `failures=2`, one of which is the new pin `test_confirmation_send_is_registered_not_performed_inline`. Then the strongest: **`dispatch_on_commit` was made a complete no-op and the whole suite run** — `Ran 1790 / FAILED (failures=5)`, and **nowhere else in 1790 tests**. All five email assertions genuinely execute their callback, so no assertion anywhere stopped being checked.

### Floors (both engines; failures read from the `FAILED (failures=N)` line)

| | SQLite | PostgreSQL 17 |
|---|---|---|
| ASYNC-2a | `1784 / OK / xf 4 / 100.00% / 8825 stmts` | `1784 / FAILED (failures=6, errors=1) / xf 4 / 100.00%` |
| ASYNC-2b1 | `1790 / OK / xf 4 / 100.00% / 8828 stmts` | `1790 / FAILED (failures=6, errors=1) / xf 4 / 100.00%` |

The 7 PostgreSQL failures are the identical pre-existing set PG-1 recorded — and are **byte-identical before and after both commits**. The whole set is named here, because an incomplete list of the very failures this floor rests on is worse than none: the 4 `varchar_pattern_ops` index-introspection failures — `OrderIndexSchemaTests.test_order_number_stays_satisfied_by_its_unique_constraint`, `OrderIndexSchemaTests.test_payment_provider_references_stay_constraint_covered` (2 subTests, `razorpay_order_id` and `razorpay_payment_id`) and `ProductCatalogIndexSchemaTests.test_slug_stays_satisfied_by_its_unique_constraint` — then the correlation-id failure `UnhandledExceptionTests.test_unhandled_exception_500_still_returns_the_request_id` (a hardcoded PK against non-transactional sequences), the pagination failure `PaginationStabilityTests.test_pages_partition_the_catalog_stably_across_repeated_requests` (the same cause), and the slug `varchar(100)` `StringDataRightTruncation` **error** `SlugGenerationTests.test_long_name_collision_truncated_to_slug_max_length`, which the earlier enumeration left out entirely. The floor is now a **pair** of numbers because the suite runs on both engines.

### Still open, and none of it is closed by this section

A send that fails is still swallowed with **no retry, no dead-letter and no customer-visible signal** (ASYNC-2c/2d); `products/models.py` still sends once per opted-in user **inside** `adjust_stock`'s locked transaction, so the request is bounded per send but unbounded in N (ASYNC-2b2); the alert cooldown is **per-process** under `LocMemCache` with no `CACHES` configured, so the documented mail-bomb bound does not hold across gunicorn workers; and `shipped`/`delivered`/`cancelled` are still dispatched as bare strings that are not members of `AuditEvent.EventType` and reach no handler.

## 2026-10-04 - SPEC-1 ASYNC-2b2 (Section 0, [R-19.11]) - builder: on all three of its call sites the restock fan-out no longer runs while the product row is locked, and the claim, not the lock, is what makes it one email per stock cycle

| ASYNC-2b2 | SQLite | PostgreSQL 17 | scripts | claim-scan |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| before (`92139d0`) | `1790 / OK / xf 4 / 100.00% / 8828 stmts` | `1790 / FAILED (failures=6, errors=1) / xf 4 / 100.00% / 8828 stmts` | `167 / OK` | `0 error(s)` |
| after | `1797 / OK / xf 4 / 100.00% / 8934 stmts` | `1797 / FAILED (failures=6, errors=1) / xf 4 / 100.00% / 8934 stmts` | `167 / OK` | `0 error(s)` |

Every figure in this table was measured in this task's final cycle, on both engines and both sides: the "before" row by running the suite at `92139d0` (both `.py` files restored from that blob), the "after" row at this commit. The 7 PostgreSQL failures are the same 7 PG-1 named above, by name and count, unchanged by this commit, and the 7 tests added here pass on both engines.

### What the defect was

`products.adjust_stock` took the product row's `select_for_update` lock and then, still inside its own `atomic()` block, emailed every armed opt-in. `orders/views.py:1752` iterates `products.objects.select_for_update()` — **the same rows** — to decrement stock, so one restock with N subscribers held a row for up to N x `EMAIL_TIMEOUT` and every customer trying to buy that product queued behind the mail. The admin request being slow was the symptom; the checkout stall was the defect.

### The shape, and why not the naive one

`adjust_stock` now **registers** the fan-out on `transaction.on_commit` instead of calling it, so the commit releases the lock before the first socket opens. That release depends on a commit hook being unable to run inside an open atomic block - Django's `run_and_clear_commit_hooks` refuses one - so the fan-out starts only once the enclosing transaction has committed and the locks it took are gone. That was checked per call site rather than assumed, and it holds at all three:

- The changelist **bulk action** and the staff **REST wrapper**: `ATOMIC_REQUESTS` is unset, and the installed Django admin opens `transaction.atomic` in exactly three places on the `ModelAdmin` request paths (`changeform_view`, `_save_formset`, `delete_view`) - none of which an action or a DRB view runs inside.
- The admin **change form** is one of those three but is not a caller: stock is read-only on that form, so `adjust_stock` is never reached there.

The **third** call site differs in shape rather than in outcome: the changelist's inline stock cell reaches `adjust_stock` from `products/admin.py`'s `save_model`, which `ModelAdmin._save_formset` calls inside its own `transaction.atomic` wrapping the whole list-editable loop, so there `adjust_stock`'s block is a **savepoint**. Releasing a savepoint neither commits nor releases the row lock, so the callback is still queued at that point and fires when *that* block commits - the same commit that releases the product row. Measured for this correction rather than reasoned from the structure, because the structure alone has now misled two readers in a row: on PostgreSQL, inside the fan-out a second connection takes `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE NOWAIT` on the locked row and succeeds, the same probe is refused inside an uncommitted atomic that still holds the lock, and it is still refused at the savepoint exit with the callback pending. Cycle 3 read this same structure in the installed Django source and concluded from it that the sends would run under the lock; the reading was right and the conclusion was not, so the experiment is published beside it.

Deferring alone is **not** the fix, and two things break if you stop there:

- **Retry semantics (trap 1).** The spent stamp `notified_at` used to commit *with* the stock change. Keep the stamp in the transaction and only deferring the send means a failed send has already spent the row: no mail, and the customer is dropped permanently. So the stamp moved into the post-commit phase, where a failure can take it back.
- **Double sends (trap 2).** The in-lock loop is what made "one cycle, one email" true. With the send after the mark's window, two rapid crossings can each reach the same armed row.

The resolution is that **the claim is the mutual exclusion and it happens before the send**: each row is reserved by a conditional `UPDATE` whose filter is the arming predicate (`active=True AND notified_at IS NULL`), `0` rows updated means the row is not claimable and the send is skipped, and a failed send releases the claim, filtered on the claimed timestamp so it cannot clear a stamp a re-arm or a later cycle has since written. One stock cycle, one email per opt-in, retry on failure, and no lock of `adjust_stock`'s own across the send on either of the two paths where it is the outermost block.

**The claim filter carries `active=True`, and that is not decoration (cycle 2).** Cycle 1 called `notified_at IS NULL` "the arming predicate" in the docstring and in this row; it is not the full predicate, and a row opted out mid-fan-out was still claimed and still mailed - reproduced in this task by restoring cycle 1's commit and running the module: `Ran 27 / FAILED (failures=1)`, `AssertionError: 2 != 1`, two sends where one is correct. The window is real because the armed list is captured **inside** the inventory transaction while the claim lands **after** it commits, so the two checks are no longer the same moment. Carrying `active=True` in both makes them the same predicate again and reuses the existing `0 rows updated -> skip` branch. Not a regression: the base blob stamps through `pk` + `notified_at IS NULL` as well, so pre-fix code behaves the same, which is why this is a P3 and not a P1.

### Tests, and the ones that can actually fail

Every send-exercising test in `products/test_restock.py` now wraps its crossing in `captureOnCommitCallbacks(execute=True)`, which is not cosmetic: a `TestCase` rolls its own transaction back, so without the wrapper the registered callback never runs and the `mail.outbox` zeros in this module would say nothing about whether a send happened. The pre-existing assertions are kept as they were, with the two exceptions named at the end of this section.

Five of the added tests were run against the **pre-fix** `products/models.py` (`git restore --source=92139d0`) and all five fail there, each with a clean `AssertionError` and no import or fixture error, so they are pinned to the mechanism and not to the outcome:

| test | what it asserts | pre-fix failure |
|---|---|---|
| `test_send_runs_with_the_stock_lock_block_already_popped` | while the send runs, the innermost atomic block is still the **caller's** (`connection.atomic_blocks[-1]` is the same object), so `adjust_stock`'s block, and the lock it holds, is already gone | the innermost block is `adjust_stock`'s |
| `test_notice_is_registered_not_performed_under_the_lock` | nothing has been sent when `adjust_stock` returns, and exactly one commit-hook callback was captured | `call_count` is already 1 inside the block |
| `test_claim_before_send_drops_a_row_another_cycle_already_won` | a second cycle claiming the rows still ahead of the fan-out yields **one** send, not two | 2 sends |
| `test_claim_skips_a_row_that_opted_out_after_the_capture` | a row opted out after the armed list was captured gets **no** send and stays unspent, while the one row still armed does | 2 sends |
| `test_rollback_of_the_stock_write_discards_the_registered_send` | a rollback that happens **after** the crossing sends **zero** mails (asserted on the count, not on an exception) | 1 send |

`test_claim_skips_a_row_that_opted_out_after_the_capture` was added in cycle 2 and is the only test that fails against cycle 1's own commit: with `products/models.py` restored from `0a9ab5a` the module gives `Ran 27 / FAILED (failures=1)`, `AssertionError: 2 != 1`, and against `92139d0` the same message among five.

Three tests are **characterisations, not guards**, and are labelled as such in their own docstrings rather than left to be read as proof:

- `test_failed_send_is_retried_on_the_next_crossing` is the trap-1 guard against the *naive* deferral. It is green against pre-fix code (pre-fix a failed send also left the row armed); what it rules out is the fix that keeps the stamp in the transaction.
- `test_transaction_rollback_also_rolls_the_spent_stamp` cannot fail against pre-fix code, because the `StockMovement` insert precedes the crossing branch, so no mail was attempted either way. The rollback-must-not-mail invariant belongs to `test_rollback_of_the_stock_write_discards_the_registered_send`; this one hands it over and says so.
- `test_rearm_clears_a_claim_whose_send_never_happened` is also green pre-fix (the pre-fix loop only ever stamped rows it had mailed). It documents that a claimed-but-unsent row does not wedge the product out of future notifications.

Two existing tests were adjusted rather than added to: every crossing is wrapped in `captureOnCommitCallbacks`, and `test_send_failure_is_log_only_and_never_blocks_the_loop` keys its failure on the **recipient** instead of on call order, because the order the loop's rows arrive in is the planner's, not the suite's, and the previous order-dependent `side_effect` list would then have been asserting something about the query plan rather than about the fan-out.

### Found, not fixed

**P2, latent test-harness defect.** `orders.tests.CurrencyBackfillMigrationTests` and `LifecycleBackfillMigrationTests` run `migrate orders 0007` / `0011`, which unapplies `products/0009+` through its dependency on `orders/0014`; the full `migrate` they restore with does not put those tables back, so afterwards `products_restocknotification` **does not exist**. Any `products` test that runs in the same invocation after them fails in `setUp` with `no such table: products_restocknotification`. Nothing in the suite was affected before this change only because every `products` test is a `TestCase`, and those all run before the `TransactionTestCase` group. The direct instrument for "no transaction spans the send" (a real commit, i.e. a `TransactionTestCase`) is therefore unavailable in this suite, which is why the lock assertion is made against Django's atomic stack instead. Fixing it means changing `orders/tests.py`, outside this task's scope and against the certified PostgreSQL floor.

**Accepted trade, stated in the code.** A process killed between the claim and the send leaves the row spent with no mail behind it; the next sell-out re-arms it. Closing that window needs a real worker with its own retry (ASYNC-2c). And `on_commit` still fires inside the request/response cycle, so this response's own latency is unchanged: on the two paths above, what is gone is other requests blocking behind our locks.

## ASYNC-2c1 (2026-10-04) — the durable notification outbox: substrate only, no site converted

ASYNC-2b2's own limit was the reason this task exists: `on_commit` releases the locks but still fires **inside the request/response cycle**, a send failure is still swallowed with no retry, and a process that dies between commit and callback loses the notification outright with nothing raised. This is the first half of the fix — the queue. **There is no worker yet, and no live call site was converted.**

### The sizing decision that shapes everything else

`dispatch` and `dispatch_on_commit` are untouched, and so is every caller. Converting a site to `enqueue` before a drain loop exists would mean the notification is **silently never delivered** — a dark regression a green suite would not catch, because the test asserting "an email was sent" would have had to be rewritten to count queue rows instead. So the substrate is landed unused and fully tested, and the conversion is ASYNC-2c3, which only becomes safe once ASYNC-2c2 adds the worker. A test class near the end of the new module pins exactly this: the payment path still delivers mail **and** leaves the outbox empty.

### What a row holds, and how it is re-read

A queue row outlives the request that wrote it, so nothing instance-shaped can go in it. `serialize_context` reduces every context value to a **model label plus primary key** for a model, and to JSON-native scalars otherwise: a `Decimal` becomes its **string** form (never a float), a lazily-evaluated translation string is forced at enqueue rather than resolved against whatever language the draining process runs with, and a `set` is described and sorted. An over-long value is **refused**, not truncated — see the cycle-2 section, which replaced this row's original "bounded and marked as cut". `resolve_context` does the reverse at drain time, re-reading each reference from the database.

**The subject is built at drain, never at enqueue.** Nothing pre-rendered is stored — no subject, no template name, no `settings.FRONTEND_URL` snapshot — so the handler receives a live row and builds the subject from current state, exactly as on the inline path. A queue that stored a rendered subject would freeze the order number and total as of the request, and would freeze the configured frontend URL too.

**A row that has since been deleted, or whose label cannot be resolved, or whose stored primary key no longer addresses its column, raises `UnresolvableNotification`** — a `LookupError` subclass, so a drain loop can catch it and close the row out instead of dying mid-batch on a notification whose subject no longer exists. This claim was **false as written at cycle 1**: it said "one defined outcome for all three" while `apps.get_model` signals an unparseable label with `ValueError`, which that `except` did not catch. Cycle 2 closes it.

### In the caller's transaction, and why that is the opposite of `dispatch_on_commit`'s placement

`enqueue` writes the row **inside** the caller's `transaction.atomic()` block. `dispatch_on_commit` defers because a *send* is external and cannot be undone, so emailing before commit would notify about a transaction that may still roll back. `enqueue` only writes a row, and deferring that write would reintroduce the exact window the task exists to remove: commit succeeds, the process dies before the callback runs, the row is never created, and the customer is owed a confirmation that exists nowhere. The insert holds no socket and adds no lock wait, so there is nothing to gain by deferring it.

**A failed enqueue propagates — deliberately the opposite of `dispatch`, which logs and swallows.** `dispatch` swallows because an SMTP outage cannot be rolled back with the transaction; a failure to write this row means the transaction cannot record what it owes, and swallowing it would produce precisely the dual write being removed. The honest cost: an unapplied outbox table turns every converted call site into a visible 500 until migrations run, which is the trade this task makes.

### The enqueue-side idempotence key — corrected at cycle 2

`dedup_key` stops the same notification being **QUEUED** twice: a retried hook, or the second of two paths firing for one event, collides on the unique constraint instead of producing a second email. Its two halves — the event type and the label plus primary key of each referenced row — are both immutable, so a retry after the order was corrected still collides. Mutable state is excluded on purpose: a key built from the order's total or its rendered subject would change the moment anything was corrected, and the duplicate would sail through.

**This cycle-1 section claimed the key was also the drain loop's "already handled" guard, and that was false.** The key derives only from the event type and the payload, is byte-identical before and after any send, and is `unique`, so it can never match a second row and carries no send state at all. What makes the queue at-least-once is the row's own `status`/`sent_at` under a claim that locks the row — a worker that dies between sending and stamping re-sends *that row* — and that is ASYNC-2c2's work, which does not exist yet.

### Floors (both engines; failures read from the `FAILED (failures=N)` line)

| | SQLite | PostgreSQL 17 |
|---|---|---|
| before this change | `Ran 1797 / OK / xf 4 / 100.00% / 8934 stmts` | `Ran 1797 / FAILED (failures=6, errors=1) / xf 4 / 8934 stmts` |
| after this change | `Ran 1819 / OK / xf 4 / 100.00% / 9015 stmts` | `Ran 1819 / FAILED (failures=6, errors=1) / xf 4 / 9015 stmts` |

Both floors re-measured in that session, before and after. `expected failures=4` on both, unmoved. `makemigrations --check` printed "No changes detected", exit 0. The `scripts` gate: `Ran 167`, `OK`.

### Tests that can fail, and how that was checked — corrected at cycle 2

One mutation was run against the new module and reverted, and it was caught by the test that names the mechanism:

- **Enqueue moved onto `transaction.on_commit`** — `FAILED (failures=7, errors=5)` at the time, among them `test_the_row_is_written_inside_the_caller_transaction`, which asserts the row is visible *before* the block exits and deliberately does not wrap itself in `captureOnCommitCallbacks`. That figure is superseded by the recorded replay below.

A second entry in this list at cycle 1 — "the dedup key derived from the serialised payload, `FAILED (errors=2)`, exactly the two dedup tests" — **did not reproduce and has been withdrawn.** Re-run on the cycle-1 tree in the cycle-2 session it gives `Ran 1819 / FAILED (failures=1, expected failures=4)`: one failure, zero errors, and it is the hardcoded key literal in the test rather than the mechanism. `assertIsNone(second)` passed, because the duplicate was still suppressed — a key derived from the payload cannot move when `total_amount` is amended, because the payload holds only a label and a primary key. The parenthetical in the original claim ("so it moves when the order is corrected") was false on its own terms. `scripts/mutation_evidence.py` existed in the tree for exactly this and was not used; cycle 2 records a manifest there instead.

A third defect was found without any mutation: the reference check in the resolver originally sat *after* the generic mapping recursion, so a reference was walked through and handed back unresolved. The first run gave `FAILED (failures=5, errors=2)`; five of those failures and one of those errors were this, and the other error was a missing field in the new fixture, not the defect. The check now runs first, and a nested-reference test drives both recursion branches — those two lines were the only part of the new code the first coverage run left uncovered.

The committed-row proof uses `TransactionTestCase`, because `TestCase`'s wrapper rolls everything back and cannot show a row that survived a real COMMIT. ASYNC-2b2 recorded a P2 in which the migration-drill `TransactionTestCase` classes unapply a table a later suite depends on. That backlog is **not in this table's blast radius**: no `orders` migration depends on `common`, and the outbox table exists before, during and after the drill. Group ordering happens to put the classes after the `TestCase` group too, but that is not what makes this safe.

Three of the new tests are weaker than the rest and are named here rather than left to read as more than they are. The pending-state test pins the storage defaults and the row's string form; it cannot fail against a wrong *enqueue*, only against a wrong default. The distinct-rows test guards against over-colliding keys, not against a key carrying a per-row nonce — cycle 2 replaced the latter concern with a real `occurrence` parameter. The non-mapping test asserts the serialiser directly, because `enqueue` normalises its argument before calling it and would otherwise be unreachable with a broken context.

### Found, not fixed

**P3, the payload is not sanitised and that is a decision, not an oversight.** The outbox payload deliberately does **not** go through the audit trail's credential-field scrubber: the accounts verification and reset emails are built out of exactly the token material that scrubber drops, and dropping it would break the mail this queue exists to deliver. The consequence is that a queued payload can hold a one-time token or personal data. Cycle 1 left the control to ASYNC-2c3; **cycle 2 rates that a P2 and ships the control now** — an `expires_at` field default plus a named deletion owner.

**P3, the table is inert until the next two tasks.** Nothing calls `enqueue` yet — the conversion is ASYNC-2c3 — so as shipped the table is empty and no notification is waiting anywhere. Once a site is converted, its rows sit undelivered until ASYNC-2c2 lands the drain loop, which is why the conversion is not part of this change. An event with no registered handler writes no row at all, so that backlog is limited to events that already have a handler.

| 2026-10-04 | ASYNC-2c1 | builder | `common/models.py`, `common/notifications.py`, `common/migrations/0007_notificationoutbox.py`, one new test module under `backend/tests/`, `backend/docs/changes.md` | Add the durable `NotificationOutbox` model and the `enqueue`/`serialize_context`/`resolve_context` substrate so a committed order leaves a notification row behind, storing only labels, primary keys and scalars and re-reading them at drain time; deliberately no live call site converted, so nothing is silently undelivered before the ASYNC-2c2 worker exists | 1819 tests OK (xf 4), cov 100.00% | frontend: n/a | shipped |

## ASYNC-2c1 cycle 2 (2026-10-04) — the substrate's claims it could not keep, and the control its payload needed

Five P2 and four P3. The auditor independently confirmed and told the cycle not to re-litigate them: no live call site converted (`dispatch`, `dispatch_on_commit`, `send_email` and the order-paid handler are AST-identical, zero production `enqueue` callers), money exact as a string with no float on the way in, enqueue inside the caller's transaction and dying with its rollback, the cycle-1 placement mutation reproducing exactly, IntegrityError-retry proven under real PostgreSQL concurrency with two barrier-synced connections, and the propagate-cost claim independently true with the table dropped. All of that is kept unchanged. What follows is the nine findings.

### BUG-1 and BUG-2 — what the key is for, and how a repeat is expressed

The cycle-1 row claimed the key was the drain loop's "already handled" guard. **It cannot be, and never could have been.** It derives only from the event type and the payload, is byte-identical before and after any send, and is `unique`, so it can never match a second row and carries no send state. The stated use was unreachable: a retry re-sends *the existing row* and never re-consults the key. What actually makes the queue at-least-once is `status`/`sent_at` under a claim that locks the row — a worker that dies between sending and stamping must send that row again — and that is ASYNC-2c2's work. The claim is corrected in the code and in the cycle-1 section above.

**The key is now for one thing only: stopping the same notification being QUEUED twice.** Two paths in this project fire for one payment (the browser callback and the gateway webhook), and a retried hook is the same case again.

**A repeat is now expressible.** Cycle 1 had a permanent, inescapable suppression: same event type, same row, same scalars meant `enqueue` returned `None` forever — so a resend after a mis-send and a second reminder were structurally impossible, with no nonce, no attempt discriminator and no key retirement. `enqueue` now takes an `occurrence` discriminator that goes into the key. Left at `None` the key says "this business event owes one notification", which is right for a confirmation; a caller that means to notify again passes something it already holds (a reminder's sequence number, a resend request id), and the repeat becomes a **new row with its own send state** rather than a mutation of the first. That is what "still owed" actually means, and it is the same thing a worker would have to key on to re-send.

### BUG-3 — a label the registry cannot parse now yields the defined error
