`apps.get_model("a.b.c")` raises `ValueError`, not `LookupError`, so the cycle-1 `except LookupError` let it escape — the single failure a drain loop filtering on `UnresolvableNotification` cannot survive, and reachable from exactly the hand-edited or migrated row that `_load_reference`'s docstring claimed was in scope. The fix is to catch `(LookupError, ValueError)` and map both onto `UnresolvableNotification`. The label is deliberately **not** shape-validated instead, because a stricter check would be worse: a multi-dot or truncated label would then fail the reference test and be handed back to the caller as an ordinary mapping, which is silent. `test_a_label_the_registry_cannot_parse_raises_the_defined_error` asserts the outcome *and* that it is not a `ValueError`.

### BUG-4 — the cycle-1 mutation figure, re-derived, and a manifest that replays

**Re-measured on the cycle-1 tree in this session:** `Ran 1819 / FAILED (failures=1, expected failures=4)`. One failure, zero errors, and the traceback shows it is the hardcoded key literal in the test (`'{"order": {"label": "orders.order", "pk": 1}}' != 'order.paid:order=orders.order:1'`) while `assertIsNone(second)` passed. So the cycle-1 parenthetical was false on its own terms: a payload-derived key cannot move when `total_amount` is amended, because the payload holds only a label and a primary key. The claim is withdrawn rather than restated.

The fix was to make the mutation test the mechanism. M2 is now framed around what a key derivation can actually break — the event type, the occurrence discriminator, and canonical ordering — instead of around a premise that could not hold. `scripts/mutation_evidence.py` was in the tree for exactly this and cycle 1 did not use it; there is now a manifest at `scripts/mutation_evidence/ASYNC-2c1.json`. Replay prints **5/5 mutation(s) held**, exit 0. Counts are read off the `FAILED (failures=N)` line: M1 `claimed=10 observed=10`, M2 `4/4`, M3 `2/2`, M5 `1/1`. M4 deliberately carries **no** count: its mutated run goes red with an uncaught `ValueError`, which is an ERROR, and the replay reads failure counts only off the failure line on purpose — an error is not a failure and must not satisfy a count. The `expected_failing_tests` names were transcribed from the first replay, after a hand-guessed set proved wrong on M1 exactly as it did at SPEC-1-B07d.

### BUG-5 — the control, shipped now rather than promised

Agreed with the P2, and not deferred to ASYNC-2c3: the accounts verification and reset mails are built from `default_token_generator.make_token(user)` and `_encoded_user_id(user)`, which is exactly the material the audit trail's scrubber exists to drop, so a security-control bypass whose stated mitigation was a later task would have shipped as design on the substrate 2c3 converts those mails onto.

- **`expires_at`** on every row, bounded by `NOTIFICATION_OUTBOX_TTL_SECONDS` (env-driven, documented in `.env.example`, default three days). It is a **field default**, not an argument, so no code path can create a row without one and there is nowhere to forget it — and the callable default means the migration stays non-interactive.
- **The named deletion owner is `manage.py purge_notification_outbox`**, in-tree and tested: it deletes every row past `expires_at` whatever its status, and never a row inside its window, because a pending row there is a notification still owed. `--dry-run` and a non-negative `--grace-seconds` are there so an operator can inspect and defer.
- Two bounds protect the token and only one is this repo's: Django's `PASSWORD_RESET_TIMEOUT` caps the token's validity; `expires_at` caps how long the copy in the queue survives.
- **Honest limit:** nothing in this repository schedules the command — no cron, no beat, no worker loop — so an operator must run it, or ASYNC-2c2's drain loop must call it on each tick. Until one of those exists the table accumulates. That is stated in the command's own docstring rather than left for an auditor to find.

### BUG-6 and BUG-7 — a cut you cannot detect, and a key that collides

**BUG-6.** The bare `"…"` marker was undetectable: an author's own natural ellipsis stored unchanged, so `endswith("…")` distinguished nothing, and the payload exposed no flag or API. Worse, the cut landed mid-token — a 233-character URL stored at 201 characters, still starting `https://` and still containing `token=`, so it rendered as a working link and failed at the customer. **Truncation is gone.** An over-long non-reference value now raises `NotificationPayloadError` before any row is written, so the failure lands at the call site. This is the opposite of the audit trail's mechanism and deliberately so: an audit record must survive its source row's later correction, so it records that it cut (with its own literal `"."` marker, which is why the two 200-char bounds were never interchangeable); a notification has no such obligation, and the only safe bound on a secret is none. Nothing in this repo puts a long value in a dispatch context, so nothing live can break.

**BUG-7.** Three measured defects in the `":".join` key, all fixed rather than deleted, because the callback-versus-webhook collapse is worth keeping. It is now a canonical JSON encoding of the whole identity with sorted keys: an unescaped separator can no longer make `{"a": "1", "b": "2"}` collide with `{"a": "1:b=2"}`; insertion order no longer changes the result, so two call sites building the same context differently still collapse; and a `set` value is described and sorted by its canonical form instead of rendered by `str()`, whose hash order varies with `PYTHONHASHSEED`, so two gunicorn workers no longer produce two different keys. Each of the three has a test that names it.

### BUG-8 and BUG-9 — two corrected claims

**BUG-8.** The cycle-1 sentence "the group ordering means a further `TransactionTestCase` cannot make that worse" had a true conclusion and a non-operative reason. No `orders` migration depends on `common`, and the outbox table exists before, during and after the migration drill; the table is simply not in the blast radius. Ordering is irrelevant to it.

**BUG-9.** The cycle-1 row quoted a `black --check` figure, which the standing rules forbid — Black is unpinned, unconfigured and invoked by no CI step — and which was deleted at ASYNC-2b2 cycle 4 for the same reason. It is gone from this row. The cycle-1 "last test class" was also wrong: the no-call-site-converted class is second-to-last. Nothing to fix in the code.

### Floors, re-measured in this session

| | SQLite | PostgreSQL 17 |
|---|---|---|
| cycle 2 | `Ran 1836 / OK / xf 4 / 100.00% / 9043 stmts` | `Ran 1836 / FAILED (failures=6, errors=1) / xf 4 / 9043 stmts` |

`expected failures=4` on both, unmoved. `makemigrations --check` prints "No changes detected", exit 0. The `scripts` gate: `Ran 167`, `OK`. `doc_claims.py --base f196aff`: **0 error(s)**, exit 0.

### Found, not fixed

**P3, the payload still is not sanitised, and that stays a decision.** It cannot be: the reset and verification mails are built out of the token material, so scrubbing at write time would break the mail the queue exists to deliver. What changed is that the decision now has a control behind it rather than a promise.

**P3, a repeat is expressible but nothing enforces that a caller means it.** An `occurrence` value is a string the caller supplies, so two paths that invent *different* values for the same logical repeat will queue two rows. That is the intended direction of failure — a duplicate email beats a silently dropped one — but it is a convention, not an invariant, and ASYNC-2c2's worker is where a shared retry key would have to come from.

**P3, nothing schedules the purge command.** Recorded above.

**P3, two new tests are weaker than the rest and are named as such in the module.** The key-shape test asserts hand-written substrings rather than a rebuilt key, so it pins that the key *names* the event and its subject, not that its encoding is exactly what it is today. The set-determinism test asserts the described value directly, which is the observable property across processes rather than a re-run under a second `PYTHONHASHSEED`. The grace-window test was a third, and cycle 3 found it: see below.

## ASYNC-2c1 cycle 3 (2026-10-04) — three prose defects, one of which had been hiding a vacuous pin

DOC-ONLY. Every cycle-1 and cycle-2 finding is closed, the mutation evidence is machine-replayable rather than narrated, and no behaviour was attacked, so this cycle does not re-run the suite or re-read the implementation. The suite was not run: the only executable file touched is the retention test class, and that class was run on its own because a change was made to an assertion in it.

- **A duplicated paragraph, deleted.** One clause sat twice inside the cycle-1 "Found, not fixed" section, four lines apart — an append where a replace was meant. The wider sweep over both ASYNC-2c1 sections then found **no other repeated prose**: the only other repeats are the two floors-table headers and the two "Found, not fixed" subsection headings, which is this file's structure rather than residue. Two duplicate long lines do exist elsewhere in the file, at two widely separated earlier sections; they predate this task and are not charged here.
- **The purge command's `--grace-seconds` help text said the arithmetic backwards.** It promised to *add* seconds to the expiry cutoff when the code *subtracts* them from now, which is the opposite operation. The behaviour was right and the operator-facing contract was wrong, which on a deletion command is the half that matters. The text now states what the operator actually gets: the cutoff moves back, so 3600 deletes a row an hour after it expired rather than at the instant it did, and a negative value is clamped at zero so it cannot reach forward and delete a row that has not expired.
- **A comment that described a different assertion than the one below it.** The grace-window test's comment claimed a negative grace must not delete a row that has not expired, while the assertion beneath it reused the already-expired row and asserted that it **was** deleted. The property is real and worth pinning, but as written an edit that inverted the clamp would have left the test green. **This cycle's own first attempt at the fix was also vacuous**, and is recorded because that is what a pin that cannot fail looks like: moving the assertion onto a live row while leaving the live row's expiry at the default three-day bound means a clamped and an unclamped cutoff both keep it, and removing the clamp entirely still gave `OK`. The live row's expiry is therefore set forward explicitly, to a minute, and the clamp is now proven: with `max(0, ...)` deleted, the test gives `FAILED (failures=1)`, exit 1, on that assertion.
- **One clause of precision in the code.** The payload is not scrubbed of one-time token material, so the compensating control is deletion on a clock, and the cycle-2 row said that control "exists now rather than being deferred". That is true of the mechanism and not of its execution: nothing runs the command on a schedule. The docstring now says so, and names scheduling it as ASYNC-2c2's, consistent with how this repo documents every other sweep an operator schedules.

Nothing else was found. `doc_claims.py --base f196aff` → **0 error(s)**, exit 0. No migration changed, and `makemigrations --check` is clean.

Reproduced verbatim against local PostgreSQL 17.11 before editing: *\"runner reported 7 problem(s) but 5 failure header(s) were read\"*, exit 2; the same real log after the fix exits 0. Fixed by tolerating the trailing parameter group and by requiring the id token to be a DOTTED path, so a parameter can never be promoted to a test id. OBS-1 [P3] a missing log path raised an uncaught `FileNotFoundError` (exit 1) while the docstring promised exit 2 for an unreadable log - reproduced, now a clean one-line message and exit 2; the same treatment is applied to the baseline file, since a missing or corrupt baseline is the identical failure mode. OBS-2 [P3] the `engine=` line was printed from the baseline JSON, so it read as measured and was not - it is now split into `baseline_engine=` (declared in `pg_baseline.json`) and `connection_engine=`, the latter measured from `connection.settings_dict['ENGINE']`, which is what can catch a SQLite log being compared against a PostgreSQL baseline. **Cycle-1's own evidence was captured through PowerShell, which suppressed the qualified id and so never rendered the subTest shape: the certification ran against an artifact of the capture method, and BUG-1 was invisible locally for exactly that reason.** A parser regression test (`scripts/tests/test_pg_gate.py`, 13 cases pinning all three header shapes, the `expected failures=4` count trap, and BOM/undecodable-byte decoding) was added so this cannot recur silently; the `scripts/tests` suite is 180 tests OK with declared count 180, so GATE-5's discovery guard still balances. All exit paths re-measured on the CI-faithful capture: new -> 1, healed -> 0 + warning, headers-stripped -> 2, ambiguous short name -> 2, missing log -> 2, missing baseline -> 2. Three subcommands: `floor --task` runs the suite under coverage on every engine it can resolve, saves the numbers to a per-task JSON artifact and prints the bullet to paste; `mutation --task` replays a manifest through `scripts/mutation_evidence.py` and prints one bullet per mutation carrying that replay's own verdict, failure count and test count; `check --base` reads the rows `docs/changes.md` gained since a ref - working tree included, so a row is checked before it is committed - and exits 1 on any floor or mutation figure with nothing backing it, naming the row. Three honesty rules are built in because each is a figure this repo has already got wrong: a green run's failure count is **absent, not zero**, since nothing is not zero; the engine is **measured** in a child process carrying the run's own environment, because `settings.py` falls back to SQLite without a word when `DATABASE_URL` is unset or malformed - the value in `backend/.env` here is a bare hostname with no scheme, so accepting it as the PostgreSQL leg would certify a SQLite run under a Postgres name; and a floor measured on one engine prints `SINGLE-ENGINE` rather than passing as the whole pair. A `failures=N` figure is satisfied by a floor artifact only when that artifact records **the same count**, which is stricter than the existence test the other figure classes get and is what lets this row quote the red engine's count from the run that measured it.
